Volts · David Roberts 2 episode(s) · 114 insights

Should we feel good about the trajectory of clean energy post-Trump?88 min60 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Lily Burmel, author of the MIT CEEPR 'Glass half full' commentary, walks host David Roberts through Energy Innovation modeling showing that after the One Big Beautiful Bill Act repealed most IRA power-sector support, roughly 74% of the clean capacity, 71% of clean generation and 67% of the emissions reductions that the IRA-plus-regulations trajectory would have delivered still show up over the coming decade — with distributed solar (95% preserved) and batteries resilient and onshore wind the casualty at 47%. She argues the surviving momentum means Democrats should prioritize zero-cost permitting reform and clean-firm commercialization over re-extending wind and solar tax credits, while Roberts pushes back that the losses (2.8 gigatons of cumulative CO2, 44 lost deployment-years by 2035) are large and that conceding tax credits lets Republicans define the space of the possible.

IRA-vs-OB3 Power Sector Scorecard 8
Energy Innovation modeling compiled in Lily Burmel's MIT CEEPR commentary finds that 74% of the clean capacity and 71% of the clean generation the IRA-plus-power-plant-regulations trajectory would have delivered still shows up on the US grid over the coming decade under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highEnergy InnovationMIT CEEPRInflation Reduction ActOne Big Beautiful Bill ActUS power sector
So what: The base case for US clean-energy deployment volumes through 2035 is far closer to the IRA case than headline repeal narratives imply, arguing against pricing developer and supply-chain equities off a collapse scenario.
“74% uh will still show up on the grid” ▶ 5:36
67% of the power-sector emissions reductions expected under the IRA plus Biden power plant regulations still occur on average over the coming decade under OB3, per the same modeling comparison.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highEnergy InnovationOne Big Beautiful Bill ActInflation Reduction ActUS power sector
So what: Sets a quantified floor on US power-sector decarbonization that survives federal policy reversal, relevant to carbon-intensity assumptions in utility and industrial models.
“67% of those will still occur on average over the coming decade” ▶ 5:43
Fossil capacity is only about 4% higher under OB3 than under the IRA trajectory, but the mix shifts toward coal and away from gas, making the fleet more emissions-intensive per unit and producing roughly 19% more emissions via higher generation rather than new build.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highcoalnatural gasUS power sector
So what: The bearish emissions surprise is coal capacity-factor retention, not a gas build boom — watch coal plant retirement deferrals and coal burn rather than turbine orders.
“the capacity in the uh OB3 scenario is 4% higher than the prior trajectory, but the composition changes” ▶ 8:44
Because fossil plants are dispatchable, flat fossil capacity still translates into materially higher emissions as existing units run more hours to fill in for clean energy that never got built.
causal🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highcoalnatural gasUS power sector
So what: Existing coal and gas asset owners capture higher run-hours and revenue without capex — a value driver for merchant thermal fleets.
“it will run for more hours of the day to fill in for the less clean energy that's built” ▶ 9:15
The cumulative ten-year emissions difference between the IRA and OB3 power-sector scenarios is 2.8 gigatons of CO2, which Burmel calculates is roughly equal to two 2024-sized years of US power sector emissions.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highCO2US power sector
So what: Quantifies the irreversible cost of the policy reversal: unlike deferred deployment, cumulative atmospheric CO2 cannot be recovered later.
“that's equal to about two like 2024 sized worth of power sector emissions” ▶ 16:26
Burmel's 'lag analysis' finds solar reaches the IRA-case deployment milestone (about 333 GW in 2031) only about 2.5 years later under OB3, but the report also shows a cumulative 44 lost deployment years on clean capacity and 56 on generation by 2035, with wind averaging 11.5 lost deployment years.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highsolarwindEnergy Innovation
So what: The same dataset supports both a bullish 'small delay' read and a bearish 'compounding losses' read — investors should specify which horizon they are underwriting.
“a cumulative 44 deployment years” ▶ 14:06
Roberts argues the honest framing of the results is that the US gets about 30% less clean energy and roughly a third fewer emissions reductions than under the IRA, which are big absolute hits even if smaller than many feared.
contrarian🗣 David Robertsconviction: highInflation Reduction ActOne Big Beautiful Bill Act
So what: A reminder that 'better than feared' framing is relative to a collapsed expectation, not to an adequate decarbonization path.
Burmel argues Trump-two clean energy policy is on paper better than Trump-one because it retains clean firm tax credits, more loan programs office funding and a decade of zero-carbon investment that Republicans passed on a party-line trifecta vote.
contrarian🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumTrump administrationloan programs officeclean firm tax creditsJesse JenkinsRepublicans
So what: Federal support for nuclear, geothermal and other clean firm remains durable through a Republican trifecta — a policy-risk asymmetry favoring clean firm over wind and solar.
“the Republicans on a trifecta you know partyline vote just agreed to create investments and spend money on zerocarbon technology for a decade” ▶ 20:39
Technology Winners and Losers Under OB3 6
Solar and batteries retain roughly 80% of their IRA-case capacity and generation under OB3, while onshore wind retains only 47% of its capacity — the aggregate 74% figure masks a sharp technology split.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highsolarbatteriesonshore wind
So what: Argues for pair trading solar and storage exposure against onshore wind developers and turbine suppliers rather than treating 'renewables' as one basket.
“we only see 47% of its capacity preserved” ▶ 11:14
Distributed solar is the single most resilient clean technology in the model at about 95% of capacity preserved, which Burmel attributes to net metering and other state-level policies plus rising utility-scale prices pushing deployment toward the distributed side.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highdistributed solarnet meteringutility-scale solar
So what: Residential and commercial solar demand is insulated from federal repeal and driven by state policy — a differentiated exposure versus utility-scale developers.
“about 95% of its capacity is preserved” ▶ 10:24
Offshore wind shows 100% preservation in the model only because its pipeline is so small that the model builds all of it, not because the sector is healthy.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highoffshore wind
So what: The offshore wind resilience number is a modeling artifact and should not be read as a bullish signal, especially given active federal efforts against those projects.
Onshore wind was already declining before the tax credit repeal because it is severely transmission-constrained, regionally limited, capex-heavy and has not seen the innovation-driven cost declines of solar and batteries.
causal🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highonshore windsolarbatteriestransmission
So what: Wind's weakness is structural, not just fiscal, so restoring credits would not by itself restore the sector's growth trajectory.
“wind was already struggling. It was already down and yanking the tax credit was kind of beating it on its way down.” ▶ 11:19
Burmel frames the preservation percentages as a two-part diagnostic: the lost share measures how dependent each technology was on tax credits, and the retained share measures underlying market momentum.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: hightax creditssolarwindbatteries
So what: A reusable test for subsidy dependence that can be applied to any technology facing a credit phase-out.
“the difference shows the impact of the credits and then what remains shows the market momentum behind it.” ▶ 12:07
Batteries retained their tax credit under OB3 while also benefiting from continued cost declines, which is a major reason storage held up far better than wind.
causal🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highbatteriesenergy storagetax credits
So what: Storage has both subsidy support and cost-curve support intact — the cleanest structural growth exposure in the modeled outcome.
What the Model Cannot Capture 11
Burmel warns that a model is a snapshot of assumptions, not a live forecast, and that anything absent from Energy Innovation's input data — behind-the-meter build, clean firm deals, executive-branch obstruction — is also absent from its outputs.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highEnergy Innovation
So what: A discipline for reading any policy model: check what the inputs track before treating the output as a forecast.
“a model is just a camera. It just captures a moment in time of what we think might happen. It's not a thermometer and it's not a live weather forecast.” ▶ 24:03
The Trump administration's whole-of-government campaign against wind and solar permits — including a July 2025 Interior memo requiring the secretary to personally sign off on public-land projects against a 69-item checklist — is a real negative that could not be modeled and was later vacated by a judge.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highDepartment of the InteriorTrump administrationwindsolarpublic lands
So what: Regulatory harassment risk is an unmodeled discount to the 74% figure, and legal reversals restore optionality only with a lag.
“a 69 line item list” ▶ 24:41
Federal-land obstruction has limited reach because 99% of US wind and 80-90% of US solar is built on private land, though wind remains exposed because every project needs FAA approval.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highwindsolarFAApublic landsDepartment of the Interior
So what: Bounds the downside from federal permitting attacks for solar while identifying FAA sign-off as wind's specific chokepoint.
“99% of wind is built on private land and 80 to 90% of solar is built on private land as well” ▶ 27:52
Near-term deployment through 2030 is protected by tax-credit safe harboring, a conclusion Burmel says is independently validated by Rhodium Group and Jesse Jenkins' REPEAT lab.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highRhodium GroupJesse JenkinsREPEAT Projecttax credits
So what: Order books and installation volumes through 2030 are largely locked in; the policy damage shows up in the post-2030 pipeline.
“deployment through 2030 is very strong and robust because of that safe harboring” ▶ 26:26
Crux Climate CEO Alfred Johnson's 'light years' framing — what gets deployed today reflects permitting and construction decisions made years ago — implies Trump-era obstruction is bullish for near-term volumes and bearish for the late 2020s and 2030s.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumAlfred JohnsonCrux ClimateTrump administration
So what: Creates a timing trade: strong reported installations now are a lagging indicator and should not be read as evidence policy damage was overstated.
“he calls the grid something like light years” ▶ 26:39
Michael Thomas's Cleanview project database shows that of roughly 90 GW of announced behind-the-meter gas, only about two-thirds have turbine commitments from manufacturers and only about 2 GW has actually been built.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highMichael ThomasCleanviewDistilledbehind-the-meter gasgas turbines
So what: The headline behind-the-meter gas pipeline is heavily overstated; turbine order commitments, not announcements, are the signal to track.
“only twothirds of that might actually have turbines and then only two gigawatts have actually been built so far” ▶ 31:18

“I think those projections are there's a large element of fantasy to that” ▶ 31:29
Three nuclear restarts — Palisades, Crane and Duane Arnold — will add gigawatts of clean firm capacity within a couple of years, and are not captured in the model's outputs because they are not in Energy Innovation's inputs.

“the Palisades project crane Duan Arnold” ▶ 33:52
Nuclear license extensions from 60-year to 80-year operating contracts represent a second unmodeled source of preserved clean capacity.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumnuclear
So what: Existing nuclear owners gain decades of additional asset life, supporting valuations independent of new-build economics.
“I think a lot of these projects are running for 60 years and we're seeing them revise their contracts to run for 80 years” ▶ 34:27
Google's framework agreement with Fervo Energy to deliver 3 GW of geothermal capacity through 2033 is cited as evidence that hyperscaler willingness to pay for speed is changing clean firm project economics beyond what cost-optimization models assume.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highGoogleFervo Energygeothermalhyperscalers
So what: Corporate offtake, not federal subsidy, is becoming the marginal buyer setting clean firm deployment — watch hyperscaler PPAs as the leading indicator.
“3 gawatts of capacity through 2033” ▶ 34:40
Cost-optimization models systematically misprice the current market because hyperscalers value speed to power over least cost, a preference that shows up as behind-the-meter gas and premium-priced clean firm contracts.
causal🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highhyperscalersdata centersbehind-the-meter gasclean firm
So what: Suppliers who can deliver interconnection speed can charge above the modeled cost curve — a margin opportunity outside merit-order logic.
“speed to power has a price that companies are willing to pay” ▶ 30:06
Demand Growth as Clean Energy Tailwind 3
The US is experiencing the fastest energy demand growth since World War II, and Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory's 2026 Queued Up report finds 90% of what was added to the grid last year was clean.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highLawrence Berkeley National LaboratoryQueued Up reportUS grid
So what: Clean technologies are the default marginal supply regardless of federal subsidy, so demand growth mechanically pulls through clean additions.
“90% of what gets added to the grid last year was clean” ▶ 38:38
The demand wave from electrification — EVs, heat pumps, buildings — will end up as large as or larger than the current data-center-driven demand pull, which Burmel and Roberts argue is being crowded out of the conversation by AI hype.
prediction🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highEVsheat pumpselectrificationdata centersAI
So what: Grid capex needs persist well beyond any AI capex cycle, supporting a longer-duration thesis on transmission, distribution and generation equipment.
“the demand from electrification, clean electrification” ▶ 39:09
Burmel frames today's data-center demand as unusually favorable because it comes from deep-pocketed customers willing to fund grid investment ahead of the later electrification wave.
thesis🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highhyperscalersdata centersUS gridelectrification
So what: Hyperscaler capital is effectively subsidizing grid buildout that later electrification demand will use — an argument against 'policing' load growth.
“thank God that right now the demand growth that we are seeing is from customers with massive pockets who want to pay and are willing to invest in our grid” ▶ 39:26
Transmission and the Deployment Ceiling 5
Jesse Jenkins found shortly after IRA passage that 80% of the law's 2030 emissions reductions would be lost if transmission could not be built faster, and Burmel notes the US has been building transmission slower ever since that report.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highJesse JenkinstransmissionInflation Reduction Act
So what: Transmission, not subsidy, is the binding constraint on realized emissions outcomes — the highest-leverage variable to monitor.
“80% of the 2030 emissions reductions from the IRA would be totally lost if we could not build transmission any faster” ▶ 44:26
John Bistline's Nature paper comparing every IRA model found that later assessments produced progressively dimmer outlooks as modelers came to appreciate transmission constraints and the deployment ceiling.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highJohn BistlineNatureInflation Reduction Acttransmission
So what: A systematic bias correction: early policy-impact estimates in this sector trend downward as physical constraints are incorporated.
“later assessments of the impact of the IRA had dimmer and dimmer outlooks” ▶ 44:59
The Energy Innovation model contains a hard cap on how much energy can be added in any period reflecting permitting, siting, interconnection and construction gauntlets, so lifting that ceiling would raise both the IRA and OB3 results.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highEnergy Innovationinterconnectionpermitting
So what: Permitting and interconnection reform is upside optionality that sits outside the 74% figure entirely.
Burmel argues better grid utilization is worth pursuing urgently but is marginal in scale: roughly 40 GW of demand flexibility capacity exists today and Brattle's cited 200 GW of unused grid capacity is only about 15% of the 1,300 GW that doubling the grid would require.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highBrattle Groupdemand flexibilitygrid enhancing technologiesUS grid
So what: Grid-enhancing technologies and flexibility are real but cannot substitute for physical buildout — sizing matters when valuing GETs and demand-response businesses.
“a doubling of the grid would be 1300 gawatt” ▶ 1:10:31
Roberts counters that grid utilization deserves maximum effort precisely because it could do in the short term what proposed gas buildout is meant to do, while the longer-term wind and solar constraints are being slowly lifted.
contrarian🗣 David Robertsconviction: highdemand flexibilitygrid enhancing technologiesnatural gasdata centers
So what: Positions utilization tech as the direct competitor to speed-to-power gas, a substitution to watch as data-center interconnection queues bind.
Permitting Reform vs Tax Credit Extension 10
Burmel argues permitting reform is the higher-priority federal lever than restoring wind and solar tax credits because it requires zero fiscal space, lowers project costs by reducing delays and cancellations, and can only be achieved through bipartisan compromise that makes it durable.
thesis🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highpermitting reformwindsolartax creditsUS Congress
So what: If Democrats adopt this prioritization, the next policy cycle favors developers exposed to permitting throughput over those levered to subsidy restoration.

Burmel says comprehensive permitting reform must pass in this Congress because negotiators are close to a final deal and the committee chairs with jurisdiction in the next Congress look less favorable to a bipartisan compromise.
prediction🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highUS Congresspermitting reform

Storage vs Clean Firm: The Firmness Argument 6
Roberts challenges the emerging DC consensus that renewables plus storage can never be firm, arguing that with 8-hour batteries, sodium-ion, new silicon anodes boosting energy density fivefold and long-duration storage, sufficient storage should eventually deliver whatever firmness gas provides.
contrarian🗣 David Robertsconviction: highbatteriessodium-ionlong-duration storagenatural gasBreakthrough Instituterenewables
So what: If correct, clean firm investment theses that assume an unbridgeable firmness gap are overvaluing nuclear and geothermal relative to storage.
“if you get enough storage, why isn't it firm? Like what is the firmness of natural gas that sufficient storage can't get renewable energy to?” ▶ 1:03:29
Burmel counters that today's 4-hour batteries are insufficient for around-the-clock reliability and that storage shifts the time of generation rather than making power dispatchable on demand, since batteries must be charged first.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumbatteriesenergy storageclean firm
So what: Defines the technical distinction underpinning clean firm investment: time-shifting versus true dispatchability.
Burmel identifies land constraints alongside transmission as a reason solar-plus-storage cannot simply scale to power everything, questioning whether Manhattan-sized solar farms for data centers are feasible.
causal🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumsolarenergy storagedata centerstransmissionland use
So what: Land and siting, not just battery chemistry, cap the solar-plus-storage substitution for firm capacity.
Burmel's report introduces a shallow-versus-deep decarbonization framing distinguishing technologies that merely reduce fossil generation from those that can supplant fossil capacity outright.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highclean firmrenewablesnatural gas
So what: A useful lens for classifying assets: peak-shaving renewables versus capacity-displacing clean firm carry different terminal values.
“shallow versus deep decarbonization” ▶ 1:05:58
Both agree the US already has enough gas capacity for a high-renewables endgame, and Burmel confirms the models do not build more gas because existing plants can simply ramp up and down.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highnatural gasEnergy Innovation
So what: Directly undercuts the case for a large new gas buildout — new gas capacity additions may be stranded-asset candidates.
“we do have enough gas, which is why the models don't build more gas” ▶ 1:06:55
Variable renewable penetration averages around 40% across grids today and can spike to 70-90% but not on a sustained basis, which Burmel uses to argue for simultaneously raising sustained penetration and commercializing the last-percent technologies.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highvariable renewablesenergy marketsclean firm

“the average it sits around 40% across all these grids and it can surge and spike up to 70 to 90% but that's not a sustained level” ▶ 1:07:48
Durable Policy Design and the International Signal 11
Burmel argues the strongest climate policy is the one that lasts, prioritizing durable consistent decarbonization results over an ambitious law whose impact is hamstrung when it survives only a couple of years.
thesis🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highInflation Reduction Actclimate policy
So what: A design principle that predicts future Democratic policy will favor bipartisan-durable structures over maximalist spending — changing the shape of the next subsidy cycle.
“the strongest climate policy is the one that can last and deliver durable consistent decarb results” ▶ 55:59
The IRA was built for 2021 conditions — Democratic trifecta, zero interest rates, jobs prioritized over inflation, minimal room for bipartisanship — and every one of those conditions has now inverted to affordability politics, high rates, massive demand growth, geopolitical competition and a Republican trifecta.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highInflation Reduction Actinterest ratesDemocratsRepublicans
So what: The next Democratic energy bill will likely look structurally different from the IRA, so extrapolating IRA-style demand subsidies into future forecasts is a mistake.
“the color of the room that we walked in in 2021, it's decorated really really differently this time around” ▶ 1:15:14
Burmel frames the clean energy downturn as a normal capital-intensive cyclical bust amplified by politically induced headwinds after the frothy zero-rate 2021 peak, and expects the next boom to be bigger than the last.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumclean energy sectorinterest rates
So what: A cyclical rather than structural read on the sector drawdown, implying eventual mean reversion for clean energy capital formation.
“the boom is always bigger than the last time” ▶ 37:04
Burmel argues tax credits are the wrong instrument for clean firm because a tax credit works best when a market is mature enough to respond to a price signal, whereas early-stage clean firm needs patient capital and long-term debt from the Office of Energy Dominance Financing.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highclean firmtax creditsOffice of Energy Dominance Financingloan programs office
So what: Points to federal debt financing rather than tax equity as the growth channel for nuclear, geothermal and fusion — relevant to where project capital will come from.
“a tax credit works best when the market is mature enough to respond to a price signal” ▶ 1:21:03
An analysis Burmel cites found solar has received 50 times as much cumulative federal energy technology support as geothermal.
data_point🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: mediumsolargeothermalfederal support

“solar has received 50 times as much as geothermal” ▶ 1:20:29
Burmel calls the methane fee underrated because its revenue sources extend beyond ten years, which made it legally hard for Republicans to repeal — an example of repeal-resistant policy design.
framework🗣 Lily Burmelconviction: highmethane feeRepublicansreconciliation

Assuming the endangerment finding is effectively dead, Burmel names four fossil regulatory measures she believes the oil and gas industry generally supports: reinstating the methane fee, expanding carbon credit support, restoring the greenhouse gas reporting program, and using that data for a carbon tariff and product-level carbon intensity standards.

The Rules That Make US Transit Expensive68 min54 insights2026-07-30
▶ Watch on YouTube
Will Poff-Webster (Institute for Progress) and Stephanie Pollock (MIT Mobility Initiative, former federal highway official and leader of the Boston Green Line Extension turnaround) walk through the Institute for Progress 'Transit Abundance Playbook,' arguing US transit construction costs run roughly 3x international averages because federal grant rules, lowest-bid procurement, pre-permitting land-purchase bans, NEPA, consultant dependence, and the 13C anti-automation statute all reward compliance over delivery. Their central structural claim is that transit is governed by far stricter rules than highways despite receiving the same federal dollars, and the fix is a specific set of amendments aimed at the upcoming surface transportation reauthorization bill. Concrete cost anchors include NYC's Second Avenue Subway at 10-20x Madrid's metro, US electric buses at ~$1M vs ~$500K diesel (30% to 2x international), and a Boston Green Line Extension bid cut from $3B to ~$2B via value-based procurement.
The 3x US Transit Cost Gap · 6Federal Grant Design vs Highway Formula Funds · 8Napkin Planning and the Land-Purchase Ban · 3Gold-Plating: Stations, Bus Customization and Fire Codes · 9Lowest-Bid vs Best-Value Procurement · 9Veto Points and the Italian Conference of Services · 5NEPA: Alternatives Analysis and Delegation · 4Consultant Brain, Civil Service Rules and the 13C Automation Block · 10
The 3x US Transit Cost Gap 6
US cost per mile to build public transit runs roughly three times international averages, and the cheap comparators are not authoritarian states but Italy, Spain, Japan and South Korea — developed countries with strong union cultures.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highUnited StatesItalySpainJapanSouth Koreapublic transitInstitute for Progress
So what: Kills the 'high wages/unions explain it' explanation for US infrastructure cost inflation and points the diagnosis at rules and process, which is where policy-driven cost relief could actually come from.
“US costs per mile to build public transit are three times international averages. And those international averages are not primarily informed by China or undemocratic countries.” ▶ 37:47
The 3x US cost gap understates the true penalty because most US transit projects are above-ground while the cheaper international comparison set includes more tunneling, which is inherently more expensive.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highUnited Statestunnelingpublic transit
So what: Like-for-like the US penalty is worse than 3x, meaning the addressable cost-reduction opportunity in US transit capex is larger than headline comparisons suggest.
“it's even worse than that because most of our projects do not have tunneling in them. They are mostly just above ground projects and tunneling projects are even more expensive.” ▶ 38:11
New York City's Second Avenue Subway cost somewhere between 10 and 20 times more than Madrid's metro system on a comparable basis.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highSecond Avenue SubwayNew York CityMadridSpain
So what: The single most extreme benchmark cited; useful as the outer bound when sizing how much of US urban rail capex is process cost rather than physical cost.
“New York City Second Avenue subway is somewhere between 10 and 20 times more expensive than Madrid's metro system.” ▶ 38:20
The few billion dollars a year the US federal government provides in transit capital grants currently funds only two or three projects annually, but would fund about ten projects a year if the US built at Madrid's unit costs.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highfederal transit grantsMadridCongressFederal Transit Administration
So what: Reframes cost reform as a ~3-5x volume multiplier on a fixed federal budget — the relevant demand variable for transit rolling stock, civil contractors and engineering firms is cost per project, not appropriations.
“those few billion dollars could either fund two or three projects a year, or they could fund 10 projects a year if we built at Madrid's cost” ▶ 1:02:16
When Cairo designed its subway system it compared American and European design standards and chose the European one because the American design cost $30 million more per tunnel with no evidence of better safety.
anecdote🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highCairoEgyptsubway design standardsEuropeUnited States
So what: Direct evidence that US design standards are a discretionary, priced-out choice other buyers reject — export markets are already arbitraging US over-specification.
“Why would we ever do the American design? It costs $30 million more for each one of these tunnels, and there's no evidence that it's better.” ▶ 1:03:45
There is now strong published evidence from NYU's Marin Institute, the ENO Center for Transportation and Circulate on US transit construction costs, but essentially no comparable international evidence on US transit operating costs, which is why the reform agenda targets construction rather than operations.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: mediumNYU Marin InstituteENO Center for TransportationCirculatetransit operating costs
So what: Flags an open research gap: any operating-cost benchmarking work would be first-mover territory and could redirect the next wave of transit policy reform.
“There is not that equivalent evidence about higher US operating costs. Um, we just don't know how US operating costs compare to countries around the world.” ▶ 10:03
Federal Grant Design vs Highway Formula Funds 8
US transit delivery is disaggregated so that no single actor is empowered to build: transit agencies lack authority, and the federal government defines its own role as compliance oversight rather than construction.
framework🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highFederal Transit Administrationtransit agenciesCongress
So what: The core diagnosis behind every reform in the playbook — watch for reauthorization language that shifts FTA's statutory mission from compliance to delivery as the leading indicator of real change.
“They are rewarded for defensive proceduralism uh and compliance with every procedure rather than actually delivering something that the public wants.” ▶ 6:38
The largest federal transit capital funding program carries roughly 60,000 words of guidance, regulation and statute that grantees must follow, versus highly flexible formula funding for state highway departments.
data_point🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highFederal Transit Administrationstate transportation departmentsformula funds
So what: A quantified measure of regulatory drag; any reauthorization that pares back statutory text is a direct cost input for transit contractors and agencies.
“There are 60,000 words of guidance, regulations, and statute for this one federal funding program that has to be followed.” ▶ 15:20
State highway departments receive multi-year formula funds with known year-one-through-five amounts and can shift money between projects at different stages, while transit agencies receive rigid project-specific grants with no such flexibility — highways get flexibility with little cost accountability, transit gets the inverse.
framework🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highstate transportation departmentstransit agenciesformula fundsFederal Transit Administration
So what: Explains why highway programs absorb schedule shocks and transit programs stall; portability of the formula-fund model to transit is the single biggest structural ask in the playbook.
Transit agencies must pay for environmental review, permitting and planning entirely out of their own funds with no federal reimbursement until the full funding grant agreement, which locks poorer communities and states out of the federal transit program entirely.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highFederal Transit Administrationfull funding grant agreementtransit agencies
So what: Creates a selection effect where only well-capitalized metros can access federal transit money — relevant to which regions will actually deploy capital under any new funding round.
“you have to put all your money up first to do the environmental review, the permitting, the planning, and you can't get reimbursed” ▶ 15:40
Highway project delivery is effectively agile — buy some land, get some permits, do early works, iterate — while transit is waterfall, requiring years of self-funded work before a single big-bang full funding grant agreement.
framework🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highstate transportation departmentstransit agenciesfull funding grant agreement
So what: A transferable mental model for diagnosing any capital program: if the funding gate is a single terminal event, the process cannot self-correct and cost overruns compound.
“So highways are agile. Transit is waterfall.” ▶ 18:05
The Federal Highway Administration has had authority for decades to grant waivers from procurement rules to accelerate innovation, while the Federal Transit Administration has no parallel authority — an asymmetry that could be closed with a single statutory change.
thesis🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highFederal Highway AdministrationFederal Transit Administrationprocurement rules
So what: One of the lowest-cost, highest-leverage legislative asks identified; a clean tell that a reauthorization bill is serious about transit delivery.
“just giving the transit authority the same legal authority that highway has had for decades to allow transit agencies to be creative in how they do procurement would make a huge difference” ▶ 19:19
The playbook is explicitly timed to the upcoming surface transportation reauthorization bill, and two of its proposals — delegating environmental review authority to transit agencies and allowing land purchase before permitting completion — already made it into the House version in some form.
prediction🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highsurface transportation reauthorizationHouse of RepresentativesCongressFederal Transit Administration
So what: A concrete near-term legislative catalyst with two provisions already scoring; the reauthorization text is the document to watch for US transit capex cost trajectory.
“both of those ideas actually got into the House version of the surface transportation reauthorization in some form” ▶ 46:01
The politically achievable near-term ask is not elevating transit above highways but simply leveling the playing field — giving transit agencies the same procurement flexibility, delivery methods and early-works authority that highway departments already have with the same federal dollars.
thesis🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highstate transportation departmentstransit agenciesFederal Highway AdministrationFederal Transit Administration
So what: Parity framing is far more likely to survive a divided Congress than pro-transit preference, so parity language is the realistic base case for reform scope.
“let's just level the playing field between transit and highways that use federal dollars” ▶ 1:00:56
Napkin Planning and the Land-Purchase Ban 3
The federal transit process requires agencies to submit essentially back-of-the-napkin plans up front and do the real planning later, inverting the project-delivery principle that early planning catches and solves problems cheaply.
causal🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highFederal Transit Administrationtransit agenciesproject planning
So what: Front-loaded planning is the cheapest de-risking available in capital projects; its absence is a structural predictor of US transit overruns.
“we require transit agencies to plan on the back of a napkin and submit it and then do all their planning later” ▶ 12:37
The Federal Railway Administration's Corridor ID program funds early planning for intercity rail, and Eric Goldwin proposes a directly analogous 'transit ID' program for urban mass transit — a precedent-based design intended to make the ask easy for legislators to approve.
thesis🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highFederal Railway AdministrationCorridor ID programEric GoldwinFederal Transit Administration
So what: Proposals with an existing federal precedent clear Congress far more easily; a transit-ID line item in reauthorization would be an early, low-drama signal of reform traction.
Because transit agencies cannot use federal money to buy land until federal permitting is complete, they buy land years after the project announcement has already inflated prices, pushing alignments into highway medians instead of dense neighborhoods — producing outcomes like Northern Virginia's Silver Line where pedestrians must cross four highway lanes to reach a station.
causal🗣 Will Poff-Webster and Stephanie Pollockconviction: highSilver LineNorthern Virginialand acquisitionFederal Transit Administration
So what: A rules-driven mechanism that simultaneously raises capex and destroys ridership potential — the clearest case where a single sequencing fix improves both cost and revenue.
“they can't get federal money to buy the land until they're years into the process. So, they're buying inflated land for the train itself. It's just backwards.” ▶ 16:30
Gold-Plating: Stations, Bus Customization and Fire Codes 9
The Boston Green Line Extension was rescued by cutting a $3 billion contractor bid down to roughly $2 billion while explicitly protecting service frequency, all seven stations, the maintenance facility and the vehicle count — everything that affected frequency and reliability was declared off-limits for savings.
anecdote🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highGreen Line ExtensionBostonMBTAMassachusetts
So what: A replicable value-engineering rule for troubled capital projects: freeze the service-output variables, cut everything else — roughly a third of project cost came out without degrading the product.
“Is it an investment that will make the train frequent and reliable? Yes. Is it an investment that will make it nicer but not more frequent and reliable? Can't afford it.” ▶ 23:29
Transit projects get gold-plated because agencies experience federal grants as monopoly money — the same dynamic as renovating a kitchen with someone else's funds rather than your own.
framework🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highfederal transit grantstransit agencies
So what: Predicts that any reimbursement structure paying near-100% of marginal cost will generate specification inflation — the same failure mode applies to grant programs in energy and housing.
“there is this sense that it's monopoly money that you're building this transit project with money from someone nine states away and so why not have the nicest transit station” ▶ 21:14
Boston's older Green Line stations in affluent western suburbs are bare asphalt slabs with at best a rain cover, yet sit beside some of the region's highest-value property — evidence that expensive station architecture is not what drives transit-adjacent land value.
contrarian🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highGreen LineBostonSomervilleMassachusettstransit-oriented development
So what: Undercuts the value-capture justification for premium station design; the frequency and existence of service, not the architecture, produces the land-value uplift.
“do we need Taj Mahal transit stations in Somerville, Massachusetts to get the benefit of the green line? The answer is no.” ▶ 23:06
A US electric bus costs around $1 million and a US diesel bus around $500,000, and each of those figures is roughly 30% higher to double the price in comparable countries — so the US overpays for both propulsion types, not just the electric one.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highelectric busesdiesel busestransit agenciesbus manufacturers
So what: Separates the electrification premium from the general US procurement premium — the bigger cost problem for bus fleets is domestic buying practice, not battery technology.
“an electric bus in the United States can cost around a million dollar and a diesel bus can cost around $500,000. And each of those numbers is about 30% more, sometimes double what it is in comparable countries.” ▶ 24:35
The federal government reimburses 80 to 85 cents of every dollar spent on a public transit bus, which is why vendors successfully upsell agencies on customizations as trivial as a bespoke bus floor unlike any other bus in the world.
causal🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highFederal Transit Administrationbus manufacturerstransit agenciesDC Metro
So what: The reimbursement rate is the mechanism — a benchmark-price cap would flip the incentive overnight and compress vendor margin on customization.
“I can tell you as someone who commutes on the bus every day, I never look at the floor.” ▶ 25:14
Rohan Res and Alex Arnovich propose setting a federal benchmark reimbursement price for a standard uncustomized bus, so off-the-shelf buses are nearly free to the agency while customization is paid for locally — with America's identical school buses as proof the standardization works.
thesis🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highRohan ResAlex Arnovichschool busesbus procurementFederal Transit Administration
So what: A specific, implementable reimbursement redesign; if adopted it would consolidate US bus demand around standard models and reshape manufacturer product strategy.
Standardized off-the-shelf procurement would buy the US roughly one and a half times as many buses for the same money, and halving bus cost from $1 million to $500,000 would let agencies run 10-minute instead of 20-minute headways — making capital cost reform a direct service-frequency lever.
causal🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highbus fleetstransit agenciesbus frequencylow-income riders
So what: Links capex reform to the operating metric riders actually respond to; frequency gains without new appropriations is the most politically saleable version of the abundance argument.
“we could have one and a half times as many buses in the United States for the same price if we just bought a bus off the shelf” ▶ 26:45
Brian Potter finds that the US fire protection rules transit agencies adopt require twice as many emergency cross passageways as European standards, with no evidence they improve safety — and these rules come from private standard-setting bodies, not from statute.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highBrian Potterfire protection standardsfire protection associationEuropetunneling
So what: Private standards bodies are an under-watched cost lever outside the legislative process entirely — reform there needs no act of Congress.
“the US fire protection rules that transit agencies tend to adopt require twice as many emergency cross passageways as Europe. And there's no evidence that these improve safety at all.” ▶ 1:02:40
Sound Transit was forced to pay for hydroelectric-dam-grade cement in a parking garage simply because a local planner did not know cement grades differ, illustrating how uninformed local reviewers impose arbitrary cost with no review mechanism.
anecdote🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highSound TransitSeattlelocal permittingconstruction materials
So what: Concrete illustration of veto-point cost: unpriced local demands land directly in project budgets, which is exactly what a single-decision-maker process is designed to filter.
“Guess what? You don't need damn quality cement for a train.” ▶ 39:46
Lowest-Bid vs Best-Value Procurement 9
Procurement is the single most important lever in transit delivery because agencies do not build — they oversee others building — so procurement rules are literally the rules for hiring the team that delivers the project.
framework🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: hightransit agenciescontractorsprocurement
So what: Reframes a topic usually dismissed as technical into the primary determinant of project outcomes; procurement statute changes deserve more analyst attention than funding headlines.
“Procurement is the rules for hiring your team.” ▶ 1:06:26
Federal law and often state law force transit agencies to take the lowest bidder even when the bidder demonstrably failed on a prior project for the same agency, creating adverse selection toward firms skilled at bid-gaming rather than delivery.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollock and David Roberts (host)conviction: highlowest-bid procurementtransit agenciescontractorsfederal law
So what: Systematically selects for contractors optimized on change orders rather than execution — a structural explanation for chronic overruns that no amount of agency skill can offset.
“mostly we make them take the low bidder” ▶ 30:04
The MBTA had to obtain explicit permission from the Massachusetts state legislature before it could use value-based procurement, showing that best-value authority is a state statutory question rather than an agency choice.
data_point🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highMBTAMassachusetts legislaturevalue-based procurement
So what: Identifies state legislatures as the binding constraint on procurement reform, and therefore the venue where reform progress is actually measurable.
On the Green Line Extension the agency published a hard price cap plus mandatory scope (seven stations, a maintenance facility) and then let bidders compete on how many of three community-requested amenities they could deliver under the cap — and the winning bidder delivered all three, including a parallel bike-pedestrian path agency staff had concluded was unaffordable.
anecdote🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highGreen Line ExtensionMBTAMassachusettsvalue-based procurement
So what: A proven design pattern — fix the budget and compete on scope — that extracted more deliverables than the agency's own engineers thought possible; directly portable to any public capital program.
“You get way more for your money if you switch from a mindset of least cost to a mindset of best value.” ▶ 32:00
Anthony Pod proposes that whenever the federal government puts money into a transit project it should require the state to exempt that project from lowest-bid procurement and allow best value.
thesis🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highAnthony Podlowest-bid procurementfederal transit grantsstate governments
So what: Uses federal money as the lever to override state procurement statutes without preemption — the most legally tractable path to national best-value adoption.
Alan Levy proposes unit-based procurement built on published component cost data — paying per foot of concrete rather than a lump sum — to eliminate the opaque up-front lump sums that let contractors extract change orders agencies feel compelled to pay.
thesis🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: mediumAlan Levyunit-based procurementchange orderscontractors
So what: Cost transparency at the component level is a prerequisite for benchmarking; without it, agencies cannot tell whether a bid is reasonable.
Colorado has switched essentially all of its infrastructure bidding to best value rather than low cost and Utah is a national leader, but states with smarter procurement must choose between using their own mechanism and accepting federal money, which forces them backward.
data_point🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highColoradoUtahbest-value procurementfederal law
So what: Federal rules are actively suppressing the state-laboratory experimentation that would generate evidence; removing that penalty is a no-cost reform.
“We should never never penalize a state that is doing innovative things on procurement by making them follow federal law.” ▶ 33:44
Transit agencies still take designs to 100% completion before putting them out to the private sector to build, which leaves low-capacity agencies to be walked all over by their contractors, while highway agencies increasingly use creative contracting and materials procurement.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: hightransit agenciesstate transportation departmentscontractorsdesign-build
So what: Full-design-then-bid removes contractor buildability input and maximizes change-order exposure; the shift to alternative delivery is where highway programs pulled ahead.
“bringing the projects to 100% design and then putting them out for the private sector to build and then getting basically walked all over by their contractors” ▶ 18:47
Asked to guarantee passage of one single reform, Stephanie Pollock chose replacing lowest-cost with best-value procurement, on the reasoning that teams will never be 100% public employees so the rules for hiring the team determine outcomes.
thesis🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highbest-value procurementAnthony Podtransit agencies
So what: A practitioner with state and federal delivery experience ranks procurement above funding, permitting and NEPA — a prioritization signal for where reform effort has the highest expected return.
“it shouldn't surprise anyone that you're not going to get the best projects if you can't hire the best teams” ▶ 1:06:19
Veto Points and the Italian Conference of Services 5
One Boston transit project (not the Green Line) needed wetlands permits from 39 separate cities and towns, illustrating how linear infrastructure in the US must satisfy tens of thousands of local governments with independent authority.
data_point🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highBostonwetlands permitslocal governmentlinear infrastructure
So what: The same veto-point arithmetic applies to transmission lines and pipelines — permit-count-per-project is a usable proxy for schedule risk across all linear infrastructure.
“needed to get wetlands permits from 39 cities in town. Okay? Not going to happen.” ▶ 36:01
Italy resolves the veto-point problem with a 'conference of services' in which one appointed actor convenes every local government, utility and landowner, decides who gets what, and closes the process with limits on subsequent lawsuits — an approach the playbook's anonymous author proposes US states adopt with a governor-appointed decision maker.
framework🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highItalyconference of servicesstate governorslocal governmentutilities
So what: A consensus-plus-finality model that avoids the political impossibility of straight preemption; the design to watch if any US state attempts infrastructure permitting overhaul.
“it's also a process where everyone gets a voice, but no one gets a veto” ▶ 40:04
Rather than preempting local authority, the federal government could condition the full funding grant agreement on all state and local permitting already being worked out — the model Seattle followed voluntarily by creating a Sound Transit-specific permitting program with a 120-day turnaround.
thesis🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highSound TransitSeattlefull funding grant agreementlocal permittingFederal Transit Administration
So what: A carrot-based workaround for the political impossibility of preemption in strong-home-rule regions; Seattle's 120-day program is an existing template.
“the city of Seattle has created a special permitting program just for sound transit projects to get them their local permits in 120 days” ▶ 36:45
American voters repeatedly approve tax increases for transit — including in car-dependent Los Angeles, in deep red states, and roughly a dozen times in Seattle — so the constraint is not public demand but a process that fails to translate majorities into built projects.
contrarian🗣 Will Poff-Webster and David Roberts (host)conviction: highLos AngelesSeattlesales tax measuresSound Transit
So what: Rebuts the standard claim that Americans don't want transit; the binding constraint is delivery capacity, which is where policy and capital should be aimed.
“It's not a process that empowers majorities.” ▶ 40:59
Asked to guarantee passage of one single reform, Will Poff-Webster chose the single-decision-maker model based on Italy's conference of services, arguing it would let politically accountable actors deliver projects in years rather than decades.
thesis🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highconference of servicesItalystate governorstransit agencies
So what: Signals that the reform community sees fragmented decision rights, not money or NEPA, as the top-ranked binding constraint on US infrastructure timelines.
“we're going to make a decision. We're going to deliver a project in years rather than decades” ▶ 1:05:51
NEPA: Alternatives Analysis and Delegation 4
The National Environmental Policy Act's core purpose is comparing alternatives, so where a project is net environmentally positive and the public has already endorsed a specific alternative through a vote or funding measure, the review adds no analytic value — the basis for Hayden Clark's proposal that a public vote substitute for environmental review.
framework🗣 Stephanie Pollock and Will Poff-Websterconviction: highNational Environmental Policy ActHayden Clarktransit projectstransmission lines
So what: A principled test for when to apply NEPA that extends directly to transmission and clean energy siting, not just transit.
“So the goal is not to get rid of the National Environmental Policy Act. It's to only apply it where it makes sense to look at alternatives” ▶ 45:00
It is NIMBYs rather than environmentalists who now weaponize environmental review to block projects, and the New York City congestion pricing environmental review ran to a bulletproof stack of binders despite being a climate-positive measure.
contrarian🗣 David Roberts (host) and Will Poff-Websterconviction: highcongestion pricingNew York CityNIMBYsNational Environmental Policy Act
So what: Reframes NEPA reform as compatible with, not opposed to, climate goals — which changes which political coalitions can be assembled around permitting reform.
“And it's not the environmentalist pushing that. It's nimbies. The nimbies are using it.” ▶ 42:51
Litigation risk imposes cost even when no suit is filed, because agency staff working under constant threat of being sued self-limit and over-document everything to ensure they would win — an internal fear effect on top of actual litigation.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: hightransit agencieslitigationNational Environmental Policy Act
So what: Means litigation-count statistics understate the true cost of the legal regime; even successful defense strategies inflate documents, schedules and budgets.
“it's not just litigation, it's the internal fear, right, of litigation” ▶ 43:18
Texas already runs the environmental reviews for all its highway expansion projects under delegated federal authority, but DART cannot run the environmental review for transit in Dallas — an asymmetry Jamie Tesler's proposal would close by delegating review authority to transit agencies.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highTexasDARTDallasJamie TeslerNational Environmental Policy ActFederal Highway Administration
So what: A concrete, precedent-backed delegation ask that already appears in the House reauthorization; also demonstrates that NEPA burden is a function of who administers it, not the statute alone.
“the state of Texas runs the environmental reviews for all the highway expansion projects in Texas.” ▶ 45:39
Consultant Brain, Civil Service Rules and the 13C Automation Block 10
Evidence points to white-collar labor — the consultant layer, not construction crews — as the larger component of the US transit cost premium, which contradicts the conservative claim that union blue-collar labor explains high US construction costs.
contrarian🗣 Will Poff-Webster and David Roberts (host)conviction: mediumconsultantsconstruction unionswhite-collar laborNew York City subway
So what: Redirects the cost diagnosis toward engineering and program-management services spend, which is where margin and headcount reduction would actually bite.
“when conservatives say US costs are US construction costs are high because of unions, I tell them actually it's a lot more complicated than that. A lot of it is white collar labor” ▶ 54:58
Massachusetts and similar states bar the use of capital budget money to hire permanent employees while permitting its use for consultants, structurally guaranteeing consultant dependence regardless of what agency leaders prefer.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: highMassachusettscapital budgetsoperating budgetsconsultantsstate governments
So what: A single, specific, fixable budget rule that drives the entire consultant-versus-staff imbalance; identifies exactly where a state-level reform would show up in project costs.
“states like Massachusetts ban the use of capital budget money to hire permanent employees and they allow its use for consultants” ▶ 49:39
One playbook author proposes a rule of thumb that 60% of staff and all of the most senior staff on any transit project be in-house, with consultants reserved for narrow specialist questions rather than managing other consultants.
framework🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: hightransit agenciesconsultantsstate capacity
So what: A measurable staffing benchmark that could be written into grant conditions; a testable proxy for whether an agency will deliver on budget.
“60% of the staff and all of the most senior staff on any transit project should be in-house” ▶ 51:18
Outsourcing project management substitutes billable-hours incentives for project-delivery incentives, because only the transit agency itself actually wants the project finished.
causal🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highconsultantstransit agenciesproject management
So what: The principal-agent framing explains why consultant-heavy programs drift; a clean heuristic for assessing any outsourced public capital program.
“if they farm that out then we're subject to billable hours incentives rather than project delivery incentives” ▶ 51:50
Transit agencies build one major project every few decades while state highway departments build every year, so transit's institutional culture is operations-centric and in-house construction capacity never accumulates — a structural rather than ideological cause of consultant reliance.
causal🗣 Stephanie Pollockconviction: hightransit agenciesstate transportation departmentsstate capacity
So what: Suggests the fix is pooling or continuity of build programs rather than exhorting agencies to be better; also explains why serial builders like Sound Transit differ from one-off builders.
Rules designed for good reasons — lowest-bid procurement to stop corruption, salary caps to avoid tabloid headlines — impose an invisible cost by convincing the public that government cannot build, exemplified by California failing to connect Bakersfield to Merced by rail over twenty years while China built a national network.
framework🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highCalifornia railBakersfieldMercedChinacivil service ruleslowest-bid procurement
So what: The legitimacy cost of process rules is real but unpriced, which is why cost-benefit analysis of individual safeguards systematically under-weights reform.
“we pay these costs invisibly and we need different rules” ▶ 53:01
Federal law known as 13C effectively bars automating an existing train line by requiring passage through an extreme veto-prone process, even though Vancouver just across the border has operated an automated train for 40 years and new US lines like the Hawaii metro can be automated from the start.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: high13CVancouverHawaii metrotrain automationtransit unions
So what: A single statute is the binding constraint on US transit automation; its amendment would be the trigger event for signaling and automation vendors in the US market.
“You go just over the border into Vancouver and they've had an automated train for 40 years.” ▶ 55:31
Research by one of the playbook's authors concludes that the New York City, Philadelphia and San Francisco subway systems, among others, would become revenue-generating for the rest of their transit systems if they ran automated rather than human-driven trains.
data_point🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highNew York City subwayPhiladelphia subwaySan Franciscotrain automationtransit operating costs
So what: Turns automation from a cost-saving measure into a cross-subsidy source for bus and suburban service — a materially different fiscal argument for cash-strapped agencies.
“their subway systems would actually be revenue generating for the rest of the transit system if they had automated trains instead of human-driven trains” ▶ 55:54
Paris automated train lines without job losses by working with the union on a plan where every driver could transfer to another line or take voluntary retirement with a good package — a protect-the-worker-not-the-position model the playbook proposes importing to the US.
framework🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highParistrain automationtransit unions13C
So what: Provides the negotiating template that could unlock 13C reform; the transferable principle applies to any automation-versus-labor standoff.
“It was a protect the worker not protect the position approach.” ▶ 56:43
US transit faces a severe bus driver shortage causing frequent ghost buses, so automation would reallocate scarce transit labor rather than create unemployment — the sector has more jobs than workers.
contrarian🗣 Will Poff-Websterconviction: highbus driverslabor shortagetrain automationtransit agencies
So what: Removes the strongest political objection to automation by reframing it as labor reallocation into unfilled roles; also flags driver scarcity as an independent cap on service growth.
“I can't tell you how often I get a ghost bus on my morning commute because there just aren't enough bus drivers employed by my transit agency.” ▶ 57:18
