Deflation, demonetization and investor positioning 6
Elon Musk predicts money will not matter by 2036 and that deflation, not inflation, becomes the central problem because the output of goods and services will grow faster than the money supply.
prediction🗣 Elon Musk (clip)conviction: highElon MuskdeflationinflationroboticsAI
So what: A structurally deflationary AI economy inverts the standard inflation-hedge playbook and stresses every debt-funded business model.
“I'll make a prediction which is that deflation will be the issue not inflation” ▶ 1:34:30
Elon's claim is better read as Star Trek economics in which food, shelter, healthcare, utilities, education and entertainment are demonetized within ten years while scarce assets — moon trips, interstellar travel, antiques and collectibles — resist demonetization, with total-economy demonetization arriving roughly thirty years after that.
framework🗣 Alex (AWG)conviction: mediumElon Muskdemonetization30-year treasuriescollectibles
So what: Explicitly flags 30-year treasury holders as exposed to the demonetization timeline and points to scarce collectibles as the residual store of value.
“For anyone investing in 30-year treasuries, I I would guess approximately 30 years after.” ▶ 1:37:52
Fiat currency systems are structurally dependent on scarcity and break under abundance: citing Jeff Booth, every dollar of GDP growth over the last 50 years came with four dollars of debt, and technological deflation means the TV factory financed at $1,000 units can never repay its loan when units fall to $500 then $250.
causal🗣 Dave (DB2)conviction: highJeff Boothfiat currencycentral banksdebtBitcoincrypto
So what: The mechanism behind persistent money printing; the stated implication is to own assets rather than cash, with crypto and Bitcoin as the pointed-to alternatives.
“every dollar increase in GDP has come with a $4 increase in debt” ▶ 1:39:43
Cash is deflating at roughly 14% a year, which is why owning assets rather than cash is the defensive position in the current monetary regime.
data_point🗣 Dave (DB2)conviction: highcashinflationasset allocation
So what: A concrete hurdle rate: any asset returning under roughly 14% is losing purchasing power on this framing.
“Cash is deflating at about 14% a year.” ▶ 1:40:24
Abundance in production does not automatically produce abundance everywhere, and money survives as a means of exchange as long as anything scarce needs allocating; the real risk is that abundance gets captured by a few big companies, which is the mechanism behind widening wealth inequality.
thesis🗣 panelist (EXO co-founder)conviction: highabundancewealth inequalitymonetary theory
So what: Distribution of AI capability, not its existence, determines whether the abundance thesis translates into broad economic gains or concentrated rents.
Individual investors can access pre-IPO frontier startups through university networks, equity crowdfunding platforms, AngelList syndicates such as Jason Calacanis's, venture funds and demo days, with syndicates singled out as the low-minimum route that has performed extraordinarily well.
framework🗣 Peter Diamandis (host)conviction: mediumAngelListJason Calacanissyndicatesequity crowdfundingventure capital
So what: A concrete, low-capital access path to private AI and deep-tech exposure for non-institutional investors.

All-In Podcast 1 episode(s) · 52 insights

The $1/Hour Robot Is Coming: Four Industry Leaders Explain What’s Next69 min52 insights2026-07-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Four back-to-back interviews from the Machina conference in Paris (Peter Funkhouser of Anyotics, Bert Borick of 1X, Amanda McMaster of Boston Dynamics, Jonathan Hurst of Agility Robotics) lay out the commercial state of legged and humanoid robotics: quadruped inspection is already a profitable industrial business priced against downtime, not labor, while humanoids are converging on a labor-priced ~$1/hour cost curve that only arrives after ~100,000-unit scale. The operators agree perception is essentially solved and that the binding constraint is now robot-control data and reliability, and they split sharply on China exposure, weaponization, and how soon 'hard takeoff' arrives (Borick's personal bet: 3 years).
Inspection-robot ROI math · 13China exposure and robot data sovereignty · 7Weaponization red lines · 51X Neo home humanoid go-to-market · 6The data pyramid and 1X's internet-video bet · 5Humanoid unit economics versus human labor · 9What is actually solved versus still blocking · 7
Inspection-robot ROI math 13
Anyotics sells quadruped inspection robots in the low hundreds of thousands of dollars per unit and justifies that price because the industrial assets they monitor lose revenue in the hundreds of thousands of dollars per hour when they go down, so a single avoided outage pays for the robot.
causal🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsindustrial inspectionoil and gas
So what: The buying trigger for industrial robotics is downtime avoidance, not wage arbitrage — model TAM off asset-uptime budgets rather than headcount.
“These assets, if they stop, they lose revenues in hundreds of thousands per hour.” ▶ 4:24
Anyotics explicitly does not sell labor replacement; its pitch is superhuman sensing — thermal cameras, acoustic microphones and gas-concentration sensors that detect micro gas leaks and equipment overheating that human eyes and ears cannot perceive.
thesis🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsthermal camerasacoustic sensorsgas detection
So what: The sensor payload, not the legs, is where margin and defensibility sit in industrial quadrupeds.
“For us, it's not about labor replacement, right?” ▶ 4:00
Robotic inspection raises inspection frequency by roughly an order of magnitude — customers who manually inspected once or twice a day now run eight to twenty missions a day, with some running missions up to 40 times a day to catch specific events such as an electric arc furnace firing.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticselectric arc furnaceindustrial inspection
So what: Value comes from data cadence, not cost per inspection — a fleet metric investors can underwrite against downtime savings.
Demand for robot endurance tops out around eight hours of runtime because inspection has diminishing returns to frequency; Anyotics robots run roughly two-hour missions with automated docking-station charging and no battery hot-swap.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsbattery technology
So what: Battery energy density is not the binding constraint in industrial inspection — a caution against overweighting battery-breakthrough narratives in this segment.
“So the maximum is in the I 8 hour range.” ▶ 5:53
Compute in industrial robots is permanently split: obstacle avoidance and data-quality checks must run on-robot in real time because connectivity cannot be guaranteed and a blurry image uploaded to the cloud is already too late, while contextual and historic downtime analysis runs in the cloud.
framework🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsedge computecloud computingGPUs
So what: Sustained demand for expensive on-robot GPUs — edge silicon content per robot does not collapse as cloud models improve.
The highest-value robotic inspection deployments are offshore oil rigs and offshore wind transformer stations, where each crew helicopter flight costs tens of thousands of dollars.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highoffshore windoil rigsAnyotics
So what: Offshore energy operators are the highest-willingness-to-pay early adopters; watch for robotics line items in offshore opex budgets.
“Every helicopter flight costs in the tens of thousands.” ▶ 6:23
Anyotics has built a robot certified not to create a spark so it can operate in explosive atmospheres such as methane-laden oil, gas and chemical sites — a regulatory-grade capability rather than a demo feature.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsoil and gaschemicalsexplosion-proof certification
So what: Safety certification is a slow-to-copy moat that cheap hardware entrants cannot short-circuit.
“we built a special robot that's guaranteed not to create a spark.” ▶ 7:19
Physical intervention — actually fixing the leak the robot detects — is not commercially available: closing levers and opening cabinets are the first steps, and reaching 99.9% reliability for manipulation in explosive atmospheres is still in development despite working demos.
thesis🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsrobotic manipulation
So what: Detect-only is the shipping product; any valuation assuming near-term robotic repair revenue is pricing a capability that vendors say is years out.
“getting to 99.9% reliability in explosive atmosphere that's still in development” ▶ 8:43
Boston Dynamics' Spot has over 500 customers across 46 countries and is, by the company's claim, the most-used mobile autonomous robot on the planet, doing daytime industrial inspection (acoustic, gauge reading, vibration detection) and nighttime security perimeter work.
data_point🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsSpotHyundai
So what: A concrete installed-base benchmark for the leading quadruped, and the reference point competitors' unit counts should be judged against.
“we have over 500 customers over 46 countries” ▶ 35:43
Spot is sold capex at roughly $100,000 for a base robot up to $300,000 in the first year fully loaded with services, integration and deployment, while Boston Dynamics expects to use a robot-as-a-service model for the Atlas humanoid because buyers will want to flex capacity up and down.
data_point🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsSpotAtlasrobot-as-a-service
So what: Quadrupeds monetize as capital equipment; humanoids are being positioned as recurring-revenue services — a materially different revenue-quality profile between the two product lines.
“It's the price of a Tesla to a Ferrari depending on how you equip it.” ▶ 38:19
Boston Dynamics measures reliability as mean time between intervention and is now above 3,000 hours, meaning a human touches the robot only a couple of times a year, with a roughly 90-minute battery and self-docking charging so a second robot covers the gap.
data_point🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsSpot
So what: Mean time between intervention is the operational KPI that determines whether robot fleets are actually cheaper than labor — use it to compare vendors.
“we're um at over 3,000 hours. So only only a couple times a year does a human have to be involved” ▶ 38:41
Boston Dynamics sells on avoided-loss outcomes rather than headcount reduction, citing a customer air leak the robot found that would otherwise have cost $3 million a day, and says industrial customers need to see ROI in under two years.
framework🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsSpot
So what: A sub-two-year payback hurdle is the gating metric for industrial robot adoption — it defines the maximum price any vendor can charge.
“customers need to see your ROI in under two years.” ▶ 36:50
Boston Dynamics argues many inspection tasks were not actually being performed by humans even when assigned, so its deployments create new inspection coverage rather than displacing existing labor.
contrarian🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: mediumBoston DynamicsSpotindustrial inspection
So what: If true, early robotics revenue is incremental spend rather than a labor-budget transfer, which changes both TAM sizing and the political risk profile.
China exposure and robot data sovereignty 7
Anyotics sources zero percent of its robot from China, a position that arose historically from local sourcing and US chips but has become a selling point for customers in critical infrastructure.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsChinasupply chainsemiconductors
So what: China-free bills of materials are becoming a procurement requirement in energy and critical infrastructure — a pricing premium and a supply-chain cost drag simultaneously.
Anyotics argues Chinese robots are excellent hardware that does not solve the customer problem, because buyers compare full solutions — autonomy, inspection intelligence, workflow integration and ISO cybersecurity certification — not walking platforms.
thesis🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highChinaAnyoticsISO certification
So what: If the moat is software and integration, cheap Chinese hardware compresses the hardware line but need not compress solution pricing — test this by watching Western win rates in industrial RFPs.
“that device is a piece of hardware that can walk beautifully. Great engineering. Love it. Do back flips.” ▶ 10:37
The security objection to foreign robots is framed as camera and data control rather than autonomy risk: an inspection robot is fifteen cameras roaming inside critical infrastructure under someone else's control.
framework🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highChinacritical infrastructurecybersecurityAnyotics
So what: Expect robotics to be regulated on the Huawei/TikTok data-security template, not on safety grounds — the faster path to import restrictions.
“you don't want to have 15 cameras in your critical infrastructure that somebody else controls.” ▶ 11:11
Boston Dynamics builds 100% of its robots outside China and Taiwan, and its interim CEO says humanoid robots from China should not be allowed into the United States under any circumstances, citing leaks from Chinese quadrupeds already deployed in the US being back-channelled to China.
thesis🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsChinaUnited Statesquadrupeds
So what: A sitting US robotics CEO publicly lobbying for an import ban is an early signal of policy risk for China-linked robot suppliers and their US channel partners.
≈ “It's not safe.” paraphrase ▶ 43:02
Boston Dynamics frames robotics as a repeat of the semiconductor loss to China and is pushing for a national robotics strategy covering IP protection and onshoring or allied-shoring of the manufacturing ecosystem.
thesis🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsChinasemiconductorsUS industrial policy
So what: Positions robotics as a candidate for CHIPS-Act-style subsidies and export controls — a policy catalyst worth tracking for US and allied robotics suppliers.
“we have seen what happens if we let China win in the semiconductor space. You know, we can't do that with robotics.” ▶ 43:10
The strategic objective stated by Boston Dynamics is platform standard-setting, not just domestic wins — ensuring the rest of the world adopts the American robotics platform rather than China's.
thesis🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsChinaUnited States
So what: Frames robotics as a platform land-grab where installed base and ecosystem lock-in matter more than near-term unit margin.
“not only do we have to win this, we have to make sure that the rest of the world uses our platform rather than China's.” ▶ 43:45
Core robot component expertise — actuators and similar parts — is concentrated in only three regions, China first plus Europe and the US, while pure labor assembly can be sourced anywhere.
data_point🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: mediumChinaEuropeUnited Statesactuatorssupply chain
So what: Actuator suppliers in Europe and the US are the chokepoint beneficiaries of any China-decoupling in robotics.
Weaponization red lines 5
Anyotics refuses military work not on philosophy alone but on product logic: military autonomy requires millisecond human-in-the-loop remote control and different communications, whereas industrial inspection autonomy assumes setup time and unattended operation, so it would require a separate team and product.
framework🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsNATOdefense technology
So what: Undercuts the assumption that industrial robot makers can pivot into the defense budget cheaply — defense robotics is a separate build, favoring dedicated defense-tech entrants.
Four years ago Anyotics co-signed a letter with Boston Dynamics and other robot makers condemning the weaponization of robots, and its CEO says the technology is not mature enough to responsibly put a gun on it.
anecdote🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: highAnyoticsBoston Dynamicsweaponization
So what: An industry-wide voluntary restraint pact is the thing that breaks first under geopolitical pressure — its collapse would be the signal that armed ground robots are a real market.
“we wrote a letter together with our friends at Boston Dynamics and others right who condemn the weaponization of robots” ▶ 14:47
Boston Dynamics maintains an anti-weaponization stance but its interim CEO frames it as business focus rather than philosophy, and says the company already does non-weaponization government work today including explosive ordnance disposal.
thesis🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: mediumBoston DynamicsUS governmentexplosive ordnance disposal
So what: A focus-based rather than principle-based stance is reversible — Boston Dynamics defense revenue is a live option, not a closed door.
“we have an anti-weaponization stance” ▶ 44:25
Pressed on whether Boston Dynamics would build armed robots if China fields them, its interim CEO declined to rule it out, saying the company would make the right decision when the time comes while insisting it does not have to decide today.
prediction🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: lowBoston DynamicsChinaweaponization
So what: Read as a soft option on defense revenue for Hyundai-owned Boston Dynamics if Chinese armed ground robots are deployed.
Armed quadruped demonstrations coming out of China are, in the view of a European quadruped CEO, still early tests and demonstrations rather than mature deployed capability, unlike drones in Ukraine which matured out of battlefield necessity.
contrarian🗣 Peter Funkhouser, Anyotics co-founder and CEOconviction: mediumChinaUkrainedronesquadrupeds
So what: Tempers the armed-robot threat narrative — the near-term defense robotics money is still in drones, not legged platforms.
1X Neo home humanoid go-to-market 6
1X commits to shipping the Neo home humanoid in 2026 but only to a handful of customers, with an explicitly slow ramp and an admission the product will be rough around the edges and the robots will fall.
prediction🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeohome robotics
So what: The 2026 'home humanoid' milestone is a vanguard shipment, not a volume market — discount revenue models that assume meaningful 2026 consumer units.
“Getting a home humanoid in 2026 is going to be rough around the edges” ▶ 18:10
1X sold out its first 10,000 Neo pre-orders in the first few days under two models — an early-adopter upfront full payment and a roughly $500-per-month subscription — and the subscription structure is still evolving alongside the product.
data_point🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeosubscription pricing
So what: Demonstrated consumer willingness to pay a car-payment-sized monthly fee for an unproven humanoid — a demand datapoint for the whole consumer robotics category.
“we sold out the first 10K in uh the first few days” ▶ 17:36
1X is turning Neo into an open platform — a consumer skill/app store plus a developer stack with fleet management, tactile-sensor data-collection gloves and in-house fine-tuning — and will allow third parties, including rival foundation labs, to run their own models on Neo hardware.
thesis🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeoOpenAIClaudeworld modelsapp store
So what: A humanoid vendor deliberately commoditizing its own model layer to win the hardware standard — the iPhone/Android question for humanoids is being decided now.
Teleoperation is a permanent product feature rather than a temporary crutch, because remote physical presence — the CEO's example of running his company through a Neo while travelling, plus unmanned remote power stations where a technician is an hour's drive away — has no autonomy substitute for rare one-off events.
framework🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeoteleoperationremote power stations
So what: Telepresence robotics is a distinct revenue line with its own economics; humanoid business cases should not be judged solely on autonomy percentage.
“Put the hat on Neo. I am Neo.” ▶ 23:08
Neo's hardware has outrun teleoperation: the new hands are more capable than a remote operator can exploit because the operator does not feel what the robot feels, and building full haptic systems would make the interface slow and clunky.
contrarian🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeohapticsteleoperation
So what: Inverts the usual bottleneck story — the constraint is the human interface, which pushes 1X toward sensor-wearing data collection and away from haptic-suit vendors.
“The teleoperator will not feel the same as the robot is feeling for example.” ▶ 24:40
A significant share of current demand on 1X's platform comes from customers wanting to automate laboratory work, on the logic that an AI model cannot advance science unless it can physically build and run experiments and observe results.
data_point🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: medium1XNeolab automationscientific research
So what: Lab automation is an early, non-obvious revenue beachhead for general-purpose humanoids — relevant to pharma and CRO capex.
“a lot of the demand that we're seeing now on our platform is people who want to automate lab work” ▶ 32:09
The data pyramid and 1X's internet-video bet 5
1X organizes robot training data as a pyramid: a small high-quality teleoperation set used only to align the model, a larger set gathered by humans wearing the robot's sensors and gloves, egocentric human video, and at the base a ludicrously immense pool of general internet video.
framework🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeotraining dataegocentric videoYouTube
So what: A concrete taxonomy for evaluating any robotics company's data strategy — ask which layer they can actually access at scale.
1X's decade-long structural bet is that making the robot mechanically similar enough to a human lets it train on all available human video, and the company claims it now has scaling laws showing this works, which is why it started its world model lab.
thesis🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XNeoworld modelsscaling laws
So what: If human-video pre-training scales, anthropomorphic robots gain a data advantage that non-humanoid form factors structurally cannot match.
“if you get the robot to be similar enough to a human then you can train on all of the available video data out there of humans” ▶ 25:06
1X argues achieving true robotic intelligence requires multiple orders of magnitude more data than anyone can collect via egocentric or sensor data in the next few years, making general internet video the only viable path and creating a catch-22 for competitors relying on collected data.
causal🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1Xtraining dataegocentric video
So what: A direct bear case on robotics startups whose primary asset is a proprietary teleop or wearable dataset.
“you need multiple orders of magnitude more data than anyone is even close to collecting over the next few years with egocentric data or with this sensor data” ▶ 27:25
1X's CEO says he is extremely sure hard takeoff — robots building robots, data centers, chip fabs and doing mining and refining in a self-sufficient system — is less than a decade away, with a personal bet of three years.
prediction🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1Xhard takeoffdata centerschip fabsmining
So what: The most aggressive timeline stated by any of the four operators; if even directionally right it reprices industrial labor, mining and construction — and it is falsifiable within three years.
“I am extremely sure that we're less than a decade away from hard takeoff.” ▶ 31:06
1X argues AGI cannot be reached without robotics because digital intelligence can never create its own physical substrate, making embodiment a prerequisite rather than an application of AI.
thesis🗣 Bert Borick, founder and CEO of 1Xconviction: high1XAGIroboticssemiconductors
So what: If accepted, robotics becomes a required capex leg of the AGI trade rather than an adjacent sector.
“the digital intelligence can never create its own substrate you need the physical part” ▶ 31:54
Humanoid unit economics versus human labor 9
Agility Robotics expects Digit V5 to run about 20 out of 24 hours a day thanks to fast charging, which works out to roughly 8,000 hours a year and about 40,000 hours of work over a five-year life.
data_point🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsDigitDigit V5
So what: The denominator for any humanoid cost-per-hour model — 40,000 lifetime hours is what turns a five-figure robot into a sub-$1/hour cost.
Humanoid robots will only reach a $20,000–$40,000 price point after the industry scales to roughly 100,000 deployed units, which at 40,000 lifetime work hours implies about a dollar an hour against $20–$40 an hour for Western factory labor — roughly 90% cost compression.
prediction🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Robotics, with host Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumAgility RoboticsDigitfactory labormanufacturing
So what: Cumulative unit volume, not model capability, is the gating variable for the $1/hour robot — track industry-wide deployment counts as the leading indicator.
“It's going to need to go through the scaling and have 100,000 robots out there before that actually is real.” ▶ 58:39
Agility argues humanoid pricing is anchored to prevailing human wages rather than build cost, so as robots get better and cheaper the price stays inelastic for a long time and the spread accrues to the manufacturer.
framework🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsDigitlabor markets
So what: Implies expanding gross margins for humanoid makers as BOM falls while pricing tracks wages — the core margin thesis for the sector.
“the value for these robots is really set by the human labor and what does it cost to pay people to do these jobs. So it's a very inelastic price for a very long time.” ▶ 59:36
Digit V5, shipping later this year, is claimed to be the first balancing humanoid that can step out of a work cell without a physical barrier separating it from people, which Agility calls its scaling moment.
prediction🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsDigit V5industrial safetywarehouses
So what: Barrier-free operation removes the facility retrofit cost that caps humanoid deployment density — the specific product milestone to watch for an inflection in Agility's unit volume.
“Digit V5 which is coming out later this year is the first time that a humanoid robot a robot which is balancing can step out of a work cell and does not need a physical barrier between the robot and the person” ▶ 56:15
Agility's Amazon deployment passed all R&D goals but was blocked because it did not meet Amazon's safety requirements, forcing a two-to-three-year bottom-to-top redesign in which every system of the robot was touched to achieve safety.
anecdote🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsAmazonDigitindustrial safety
So what: Safety certification, not capability, is the hidden multi-year gate between a working humanoid demo and enterprise revenue — a timing risk for every new humanoid entrant.
Agility argues humanoids are the wrong form factor for fully lights-out package sortation, where purpose-built specialty automation running 24/7 is more efficient, and that humanoids win specifically in legacy and human-designed spaces such as retail, grocery, hospitals, construction sites and doorstep delivery.
contrarian🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility Roboticswarehouse automationretailgroceryhospitalsconstruction
So what: Splits the automation TAM: fixed automation keeps greenfield warehouses while humanoids monetize brownfield and human-facing environments — different competitive sets and margins.
“And a humanoid doesn't make sense. It's not the most efficient thing for that specific task. A humanoid is useful for walking into human environments doing human workflows.” ▶ 1:01:39
Agility demonstrated last-mile delivery roughly seven years ago with Ford — a Digit robot exiting a vehicle, walking up stairs to a front porch and dropping a package — but has deliberately deprioritized it because warehouse work is the bigger first market.
anecdote🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsFordDigitlast-mile delivery
So what: Capability for robotic last-mile delivery has existed for years; the constraint is market prioritization, so a combined robotaxi-plus-humanoid delivery stack could appear faster than expected once economics justify it.
Agility argues the majority of workers in many warehouse and sortation applications are already robots — AMRs, conveyor belts and industrial arms — so humanoids are an incremental new form of automation rather than the start of displacement.
contrarian🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsAMRswarehouse automationAmazon
So what: Reframes the humanoid displacement debate — the marginal automation dollar competes against existing fixed automation, not against headcount.
Agility frames robotics as the only route to US GDP growth given a non-growing population and anti-immigration politics, since growth must come from increased efficiency and capability rather than more workers.
thesis🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Robotics, with host Jason Calacanisconviction: mediumUnited StatesGDPimmigrationproductivity
So what: The macro argument that gives robotics political cover and potential policy support in the US despite labor-displacement optics.
What is actually solved versus still blocking 7
Agility's chief robot officer says perception is all but solved and calls it the huge inflection point, because large language models now give robots free semantic understanding of a scene that three or four years ago required narrow hand-programming.
thesis🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility Roboticslarge language modelscomputer vision
So what: Perception is now commoditized input rather than differentiation — value in robotics is migrating to control, data and safety certification.
“the fact that perception is all but solved at this point is a really, really huge inflection point” ▶ 49:58
The binding constraint in robotics is that no internet-scale training set exists for robot control — there is no corpus of torque commands to every motor given all sensor input — so unlike language models the data must be manufactured.
causal🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility Roboticslarge language modelsrobot controltraining data
So what: Explains why robotics will not simply ride the LLM curve and why data-generation methods (sim, teleop, video pre-training) are the real competitive axis.
“these language models are trained off of the entire data on the internet and that data does not exist for robot control” ▶ 51:20
Agility rejects the idea that world models are a silver bullet, citing a persistent sim-to-real gap from unmodelled physics such as wave dynamics in a liquid and condensation on a glass, which still requires real practice with real robots.
contrarian🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility Roboticsworld modelssimulationsim-to-real
So what: A direct counterweight to simulation-first robotics narratives — physical fleet hours retain strategic value even in a world-model era.
Agility explicitly rejects a robotics singularity in favor of a snowball model of compounding progress driven by capital, engineering time and parallel exploration of many tools rather than any single breakthrough.
framework🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility RoboticsAI progress
So what: The most direct disagreement in the episode with 1X's three-year hard-takeoff call — bracket robotics timelines rather than anchoring to any single operator's forecast.
“Think of it more like a snowball picking up steam going down a hill.” ▶ 54:25
Robots remain far worse learners than humans, requiring vastly more data and examples, but they have a structural offsetting advantage: skills learned by one robot transfer over the network to all robots of that type.
framework🗣 Jonathan Hurst, co-founder and chief robot officer at Agility Roboticsconviction: highAgility Roboticsfleet learningrobot learning
So what: Fleet learning turns installed base into a compounding capability moat — favors whoever deploys the most units of a single hardware type first.
“Robots take so much more data, so many more examples than a person.” ▶ 54:49
Boston Dynamics splits robot intelligence into two brains — the physical control brain that stays on the robot, and a cloud-capable semantic reasoning layer it may source through partners such as Google DeepMind — with a customer-specific workflow wrapper living in between and placed on-robot or in cloud as needed.
framework🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsAtlasGoogle DeepMindcloud computing
So what: Signals that hardware leaders intend to buy rather than build the reasoning layer, creating an addressable market for foundation-model providers in robotics.
Humanoid battery constraints are being engineered away at the robot level rather than the cell level: Boston Dynamics' Atlas carries two batteries and swaps them itself by rotating its torso, so it never fully powers down, while quadrupeds simply dock and charge.
data_point🗣 Amanda McMaster, interim CEO of Boston Dynamicsconviction: highBoston DynamicsAtlasSpotbatteries
So what: Self-swapping batteries push effective humanoid uptime toward 20+ hours a day, which is what makes the sub-$1/hour cost math work.

Bankless 1 episode(s) · 51 insights

Morpho Just Removed DeFi's Most Important Number63 min51 insights2026-07-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Paul of Morpho explains Morpho Midnight, a fixed-rate/fixed-term lending protocol that replaces DeFi's algorithmic interest rate model with tradeable zero-coupon obligations priced by an on-chain order book, arguing this is what institutions like Apollo demand and the only true financing primitive. He targets >$10B and possibly $100B in Midnight TVL by end-2027, frames Morpho's mission as compressing the 200-300bps net interest margin the financial system extracts, and discusses Hester Peirce's warning that some DeFi vaults may be investment companies.
Zero-Coupon Obligations as the True Lending Primitive · 6DeFi Layering and Externalizing Rate + Risk · 6Institutional Rejection of the Interest Rate Model · 5Pricing Trust and Undercollateralized Credit · 6Midnight Order Book and Launch Liquidity Strategy · 6Asset Mix, TAM and TVL Targets · 7Net Interest Margin Compression and the Everything-Is-a-Neobank Thesis · 7Hester Peirce, Vault Liability and the Curator Question · 8
Zero-Coupon Obligations as the True Lending Primitive 6
Morpho's next-generation protocol, Morpho Midnight, replaces the algorithmic interest rate model of Morpho Blue with fixed-rate, fixed-term lending built on tradeable zero-coupon obligations whose market price determines the rate.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphoMorpho MidnightMorpho BlueDeFi lending
So what: The dominant on-chain lending protocol is deprecating the utilization-curve rate model that every major DeFi lender uses, which would reset how on-chain credit is priced and benchmarked.
“Morpho Midnight is basically the next version of Morpho uh after Morpho Blue.” ▶ 0:18
Paul argues fixed-rate fixed-term is the only true financing primitive because a variable rate must depend on some arbitrary formula or governance decision, whereas a zero-coupon obligation depends on nothing external for its existence.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightzero-coupon obligationDeFi lending
So what: If the market accepts this framing, variable-rate pool lending gets re-rated as a legacy retail wrapper rather than the base layer of on-chain credit.
“A primitive does not rely on anything for its existence.” ▶ 9:38
A Morpho Midnight market is constructed from a collateral asset, a loan asset, an oracle, and a maturity term, substituting the term for Morpho Blue's interest rate model (the utilization curve).
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightMorpho Blueoracles
So what: Anyone modeling Midnight risk should note the parameter set is nearly identical to Blue's except the rate is externalized to market pricing rather than encoded.
In Morpho Midnight, a 5% one-year loan is expressed as an obligation issued at 95 that settles at 1 (par) at maturity, with the discount to par defining the interest rate.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightzero-coupon obligation
So what: Rates on Midnight will be read off discount pricing rather than a posted APY, so yield trackers and integrators need bond-style math to compare it to Aave/Morpho Blue.
Morpho's team frames itself as computer scientists whose goal is to remove every financial component from the infrastructure layer and push pricing decisions out to the market.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphoMorpho Midnight
So what: A protocol design philosophy of minimal embedded finance means fewer governance levers, which matters for both regulatory posture and where value accrues in the stack.
“we as Moro are computer scientists. We're not finance people. We want every financial component to be removed from the infrastructure.” ▶ 16:03
Morpho Midnight solves liquidity fragmentation by letting a single posted liquidity position back borrowing across 10,000 isolated markets simultaneously, which Paul identifies as the zero-to-one feature that earlier fixed-term attempts lacked.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightETHLendDharma
So what: This is the mechanism that could make fixed-term lending viable at scale where peer-to-peer designs historically failed on thin per-market liquidity.
“you can like post one liquidity across 10,000 markets that are isolated and your liquidity will be available to borrow in all of those markets at the same time.” ▶ 14:25
DeFi Layering and Externalizing Rate + Risk 6
Paul's model of DeFi evolution is progressive modularization: protocols initially internalized risk and rate management because gas was expensive and users were unsophisticated MetaMask holders, and each successive generation externalizes another responsibility to specialized market participants.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highUniswapCompoundAaveMorpho BlueMorpho MidnightMetaMask
So what: The framework predicts where value migrates next: away from monolithic protocols toward curators, market makers, and specialized intermediaries.
“DeFi is layering and that's a good thing in my opinion it's just going to provide us much more flexibility and much more market efficiency” ▶ 5:35
Paul maps the same modularization arc onto Uniswap, where v2 was passive for both LPs and traders, v3 externalized LP management, and v4 made both direct taking and direct LPing hard, pushing activity to MEV bots and market makers.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highUniswapUniswap v2Uniswap v3Uniswap v4MEV botsmarket makers
So what: Confirms that protocol upgrades systematically shift profit from passive users to professional intermediaries, a pattern worth pricing into any DeFi token thesis.
Paul argues DeFi trading reached a clean primitive faster than DeFi lending because trading is a lower-dimensional problem while lending risk is multi-dimensional and therefore requires active management.
causal🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highUniswapDeFi lendingDeFi trading
So what: Explains why lending will keep needing a human/curator layer even as it modularizes, so the curator role is structural rather than transitional.
“lending involves risk and risk is multi-dimensional which you know requires some management” ▶ 13:52
Earlier fixed-rate peer-to-peer lending attempts like Dharma and ETHLend failed for three reasons: prohibitive gas costs, the absence of a deep enough set of market participants to make markets active, and flawed designs.
causal🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highDharmaETHLendEtherDeltaCompound
So what: The bear case on Midnight is that liquidity again fails to materialize, so tracking participant count and depth is the key falsification test.
In Morpho Midnight, vaults remain the retail-facing layer, aggregating and rolling zero-coupon obligations on the depositor's behalf so the end user experience looks identical to a Morpho Blue vault.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightMorpho Bluevaultscurators
So what: Retail deposit flow does not need to understand bonds, meaning Midnight adoption can scale without user education — and curator quality becomes the differentiator.
Midnight lets curators control both rate and liquidity, so a vault can specify a minimum lending rate (e.g. not below 5% for a Bitcoin-backed market) and choose to lock capital for six months to achieve higher capital utilization or stay liquid instead.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightcuratorsBitcoinvaults
So what: Curators gain a duration/liquidity dial they never had in pool lending, which should produce visible dispersion in vault yields based on curator skill.
Institutional Rejection of the Interest Rate Model 5
Roughly half of the largest traditional financial institutions Paul has pitched told him the DeFi interest rate model is the most stupid thing they have ever seen, because portfolio managers refuse to have their rate dictated by a formula or a DAO.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphotraditional financial institutionsDAOs
So what: This is the demand-side evidence that variable-rate pool lending has a hard ceiling with institutional capital, capping the TAM of Aave-style designs.
“And like pretty much half of them are like this interest rate model thing is the most stupid thing we've ever seen.” ▶ 12:51
Apollo, described by Paul as the largest private credit fund in the world and among the most complex portfolio managers, is investing in Morpho and represents the class of user that will not accept DAO-set rates.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highApolloMorphoprivate credit
So what: A named large private-credit anchor validates the institutional thesis and is a concrete counterparty to watch for early Midnight deployment.
“Apollo is like the largest private credit fund in in the world. They're you know in you know investing in in Morpho.” ▶ 12:27
Paul predicts every large institution with a crypto arm that has not already touched Morpho Midnight will do so within 24 months, and says some counterparties have already moved past proof-of-concept into launching entire businesses.
prediction🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightinstitutionscrypto arms
So what: A dated, falsifiable institutional adoption claim; the shift out of POCs into live businesses is the leading indicator to track.
“all the large ones that have a crypto arm if they haven't touched midnight already they will in the next 24 months” ▶ 37:36
Paul lays out a fixed adoption sequence for on-chain finance: wallets first, then exchanges, then fintechs, then neobanks, then asset managers, and finally banks, with the most aggressive player in each bucket moving first and forcing conservative incumbents to follow.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highwalletsexchangesfintechsneobanksasset managersbanks
So what: Gives a sequencing map for where to expect the next integration announcements and which category is currently at the frontier.
“the order of things has always been like wallets first then exchanges then fintex then neo bankanks then asset managers and then the actual banks” ▶ 38:08
Paul argues banks and asset managers now have no strategic choice but to lean into on-chain rails, because the game theory of some competitors already leaning in removes the option of blocking, giving DeFi more leverage over TradFi than people assume.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highbanksasset managersDeFiTradFi
So what: Implies incumbent adoption accelerates non-linearly once a first mover appears in each category rather than proceeding gradually.
“I think actually DeFi has much more leverage on Treadfi now than than people may think.” ▶ 44:38
Pricing Trust and Undercollateralized Credit 6
Morpho Midnight includes a general-purpose module that lets a borrower post arbitrary on-chain signals about why they should be trusted — collateral, identity, receivables, business documents, or a ZK proof — which curators then assess and price.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightcuratorsZK proofsidentityreceivables
So what: This is the optional path from overcollateralized crypto loans to real credit underwriting, and the module's usage rate is the metric that would signal it working.
“there's a module in the protocol that lets you express why you should be trusted.” ▶ 29:26
Paul argues undercollateralized lending was never solvable by a smart contract underwriting credit, and the actual unlock is externalizing both rate and risk so that specialized underwriters such as the largest private credit funds can price any type of risk.
contrarian🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightundercollateralized lendingprivate credit funds
So what: Reverses six years of DeFi consensus that protocols must build credit scoring; it means credit expertise, not protocol code, is the scarce input.
“a piece of code is not here to to give credit to people like credit is complex however maybe the largest private credit funds in the world know how to underwrite those things” ▶ 31:17
Morpho is deliberately not launching the trust/attestation module first, starting Midnight instead with overcollateralized crypto loans while the general module remains technically available.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightovercollateralized loans
So what: Tempers near-term expectations for on-chain uncollateralized credit volume even though the plumbing exists.
“we're not focusing on that for now we're focusing it's something that's possible in the protocol” ▶ 32:24
Paul predicts Morpho Midnight will be the first product-market fit for the on-chain identity layer, because identity previously lacked a business case and now delivers a measurable few-basis-point reduction in cost of capital.
prediction🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnighton-chain identitycost of capital
So what: Gives a concrete demand driver for identity/attestation infrastructure, a sector that has struggled to monetize.
“Morph Midnight is going to be the first PMF of the identity layer.” ▶ 35:18
Paul frames on-chain lending's value as the matching layer only — running a global auction across N lenders rather than accepting one bank's rate — with legal enforcement of any off-chain claim remaining outside the protocol.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphomortgagesbanksauctions
So what: Clarifies the realistic scope of protocol liability and where off-chain legal infrastructure must still be built for real-world credit.
“you're going to run an auction on 10,000 different banks” ▶ 34:24
Paul describes Morpho's mission as collapsing the cost of trust — the cost at which humans must believe in one another — by providing open rails on which lenders compete to offer the best terms.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphocost of trust
So what: The mission framing signals that Morpho will keep prioritizing pricing efficiency over feature breadth, useful for anticipating roadmap.
“I think of Morph's mission is basically collapsing the cost of trust” ▶ 47:27
Midnight Order Book and Launch Liquidity Strategy 6
Morpho Midnight runs as an order book with exactly four order types — make borrow, take borrow, make lend, and take lend — and treats primary issuance and secondary trading as the same construct, so a lender exiting early can match with a counterparty who wanted that shorter maturity.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightorder bookcurators
So what: Unified primary/secondary means duration risk is tradeable rather than locked, which is what makes fixed-term acceptable to liquidity-sensitive lenders.
“in more for midnight you can make make bar offers, take bar offers, uh make land offers and take land offers. You have really four types of orders.” ▶ 24:29
Obligations for a given Morpho Midnight market are fungible in the same way aTokens are, so each market is a genuine pooled market rather than a series of bilateral OTC agreements, with additional transversal network effects from multi-market offers and callbacks.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightAaveaTokensBTC/USDC
So what: Addresses the core objection that fixed-term lending is too bilateral to produce real market-clearing prices.
Morpho Midnight's liquidity bootstrap is a single code parameter in Morpho vaults that, once voted on, would let billions of dollars of existing Morpho vault liquidity allocate into Midnight, which Paul says would bring ten-figure scale pretty easily.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho MidnightMorpho vaultsMorpho governance
So what: The vault-allocation switch is the single highest-impact catalyst to watch for Midnight TVL; it is a discrete governance event, not a gradual ramp.
“Midnight has billions of dollars already accessible.” ▶ 19:53
Morpho is deliberately slowing the Midnight launch to let market makers, lenders and borrowers integrate the code and APIs first, with Paul citing that DeFi has been rough for the last six months as a reason for caution.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightmarket makersDeFi
So what: Sets expectations that Midnight TVL stays small near-term by design, so early low numbers are not evidence of failure.
“We want the launch of more midnight to be as slow as possible.” ▶ 20:19
Within 10 hours of Midnight's release, five or six public threads had already plotted its rate curve despite the protocol holding only about 100k of liquidity, which Paul warned was small enough for anyone to manipulate.
anecdote🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho Midnightrate curve
So what: Early Midnight rate-curve data circulating publicly is not yet a reliable signal; treat any yield-curve inference from it as noise until liquidity deepens.
“we released Midnight and then literally 10 hours later you had already like five or six threads on like the rate curve” ▶ 27:58
Paul says price discovery on native interest rates for assets beyond stablecoins has little short-term business value for Morpho and is more of a personal intellectual interest than a strategic priority.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: mediumMorpho Midnightinterest rate curvesstablecoins
So what: Suggests ETH/BTC yield-curve formation on Midnight will be an emergent byproduct rather than a promoted product, so expect slower development there.
“I don't think it has so much business value for Moro in the short term. So it's like I need to like it's a bit like a nerd like interest for me.” ▶ 28:39

Net Interest Margin Compression and the Everything-Is-a-Neobank Thesis 7
Paul estimates the financial system's net interest margin costs the entire economy roughly 200-300 basis points, and says open on-chain competition will compress it — which is why Morpho expects to be both hated and loved.
thesis🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highbanksnet interest marginMorpho
So what: Quantifies the disruption pool at stake; bank NIM is the revenue line most directly threatened if on-chain financing scales.
“we should account for like 200 300 bips on the entire economy” ▶ 43:17
Paul argues openness produces two concrete economic benefits — better pricing from fierce global competition and easier integration from open-source code — and that the resulting crush in the lend/borrow spread is the killer use case of crypto financing.
causal🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorphoCoinbaseopen source
So what: Frames spread compression, not yield, as the durable competitive advantage of on-chain lending over centralized desks.
“the spread between the lending rate and the borrowing rate is going to be crushed in a crypto environment because everything is so open” ▶ 22:07
Coinbase's DeFi borrow product routed to Morpho delivers materially better rates than a centralized desk because tens of thousands of global lenders compete to fill the same demand.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highCoinbaseMorphocentralized lending desks
So what: A live proof point that on-chain matching beats CeFi pricing, which is the argument that pulls other distribution platforms in.
“the DeFi borrow product of Coinbase delivers much better rates than what you would get if you were to use a centralized desk for the same financing opportunity” ▶ 21:55
Paul predicts anyone with a large surface of user interaction will financialize their user base via embedded self-custodial wallets from providers like Dynamic, Turnkey or Privy, so consumer apps such as Uber or Airbnb become de facto neobanks with a distribution edge over dedicated neobanks.
prediction🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highDynamicTurnkeyPrivyUberAirbnbneobanksMorpho
So what: Implies embedded-wallet infrastructure and lending backends capture the value while consumer-brand distribution, not fintech branding, wins the front end.
“anybody that has distribution now will think about financializing their user base” ▶ 39:49

“it became the easiest way to build like a financial app is on cryptos” ▶ 41:59

Hester Peirce, Vault Liability and the Curator Question 8
Hester Peirce recently stated that DeFi vaults may be investment companies or investment contracts and that lending strategies may create notes that are securities, which Paul says did not surprise him and which he found reasonable and thoughtful.
data_point🗣 David (host, Bankless) and Paul (Morpho)conviction: highHester PeirceSECDeFi vaultssecurities law
So what: Direct regulatory signal that vault products sit on a securities-law spectrum; curator-run discretionary vaults carry the most exposure.
“vaults may be investment companies or investment contracts and she also said that lending strategies may create notes that are securities” ▶ 52:16
Paul spent an entire week in Washington meeting the SEC, the CFTC, senators and staffers, and characterizes regulators as very open over recent months.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highSECCFTCUS CongressMorpho
So what: Morpho is actively shaping the vault rulemaking conversation, which reduces the tail risk of an adverse surprise for its stack specifically.
Paul argues the key regulatory insight is that vaults exist on a spectrum of custodiality and agency — some are fully immutable and non-custodial with time-locked risk parameters, while others are effectively just a Fireblocks wallet that manages everything.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highDeFi vaultsFireblocksSECcurators
So what: A usable diligence axis for vault risk: check whether parameters are time-locked and immutable or discretionary and custodial.
“some of them are fully non-custodial like fully immutable where you can't change the risk parameters within the bounds of a time lock and some others are frankly just Fireblocks wallet, right?” ▶ 53:05
Morpho vaults are structurally constrained so that a vault can only deposit into Morpho markets and never into other vaults, and curators can only add markets after a time lock during which depositors can withdraw in stablecoins or in kind.
data_point🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: highMorpho vaultscuratorstime locks
So what: This is the concrete safety property differentiating Morpho vaults from recursive vault-of-vault structures that concentrate hidden risk.
“a morpho vault can't deposit into other vaults. A morph vault can only deposit into morpho markets.” ▶ 58:07
Paul divides vault-sector responsibility among three actors — the distributor, the curator, and the infrastructure — with distributors obliged to disclose risk because they choose the single option shown to users, and curators liable in proportion to their discretion over asset allocation.
framework🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: mediumdistributorscuratorsMorphovault infrastructure
So what: A liability map for the vault stack that investors can use to anticipate where enforcement or registration requirements land first.
“If you have full discretion on where the asset goes, then it's hard to think that if you're in control, then you're not liable, right?” ▶ 59:39
The host argues that no one in the vault vertical currently takes liability and that someone — most plausibly curators, who should also capture more upside and skin in the game — must become registered and compliant for the sector to scale 100x safely.
thesis🗣 David (host, Bankless)conviction: mediumcuratorsvaultsSECHester Peirce
So what: Predicts a coming bifurcation into regulated curators capturing higher fees versus unregulated ones, a structural shift in where vault economics accrue.
“somebody in the vault vertical needs to take the liability” ▶ 57:06
Paul says he coined the term curator deliberately as a vague new word because the role did not map cleanly onto asset management, and expects vocabulary to be refined with qualifying adjectives once regulators draw lines.
anecdote🗣 Paul (Morpho)conviction: mediumcuratorsSECregulatory taxonomy
So what: Terminology ambiguity in DeFi is often intentional; expect naming changes to accompany regulatory classification, which can shift how products are marketed and sold.
“frankly, that's, you know, when I came up with the word curator” ▶ 54:55
The host cites Andrew Hong's startup, which uses AI to inspect vault topology, as evidence that a depositor chasing 6% in one USDC vault may in fact be exposed to 17 downstream vaults, describing the interconnection as frankly scary.
anecdote🗣 David (host, Bankless)conviction: mediumAndrew HongUSDCvaultsAI
So what: Hidden vault-of-vault recursion is a systemic contagion vector; tooling that maps it is becoming a necessary diligence input for yield allocators.

The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

Why Abundance Is an Illusion with Jeff Currie | TGS 22988 min50 insights2026-07-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Recorded July 23, 2026, former Goldman Sachs commodities head Jeff Currie argues that refined products, not crude, are where the real shortage already is: the 3-2-1 crack spread at $60/bbl is the highest of his three-decade career after strikes took out roughly half of Russian refining and the Strait of Hormuz, Red Sea and Black Sea were all closed. He frames Western policy as an 'abundance illusion' - draining strategic reserves and talking markets down to avoid ever admitting scarcity - which suppresses the price signal needed to trigger investment, and argues the Bretton Woods 'grand bargain' of US-protected sea lanes for dollar usage is breaking, ending globalization and forcing a return to state capitalism. His portfolio conclusion is a rotation between scarcity (oil, copper, ags) and debasement (gold), with capital only arriving after physical shortages appear.
Diesel and the Refined-Product Shortage · 7Hormuz Complacency and the Escalation Trap · 6The Physical Capital Paradox and State Capitalism · 7The Abundance Illusion Playbook · 4Grand Bargain, Exorbitant Privilege and Bloc Fragmentation · 6Scarcity Versus Debasement: The Second Supercycle · 7Fiscal Transfers, Credit and Sovereign Fragility · 6War Kit 2.0 and the Rise of the Electron State · 7
Diesel and the Refined-Product Shortage 7
The 3-2-1 crack spread (three parts gasoline, two diesel, one crude) reached $60 a barrel, the highest level Jeff Currie has seen in three decades of covering commodities, implying products priced as if crude were $160 while crude trades near $100.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcrack spreaddieselgasolinecrude oilBrent
So what: Refining margins, not flat crude, are where the physical tightness is being expressed - refiner equities and product spreads are the cleaner expression of the shock than long crude.
≈ “It's sixty dollars a barrel That's the highest level in the three decades I've been doing this.” paraphrase
Currie treats refined product prices as the true signal of physical tightness and crude as noise, arguing the macro community is watching the wrong instrument.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcrude oilrefined productsdiesel
So what: A reusable screen: when crude and products diverge, position off product cracks and inventories rather than headline Brent/WTI.
≈ “I like to say crude is the noise, products are the signal, but their people are not looking at them.” paraphrase
Diesel products are up roughly 81% year to date versus about 34% for the broad commodity index, making diesel the current bottleneck after oil earlier in the year.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highdieselcommodity index
So what: The bottleneck rotates within commodities while the return trend persists - track which sub-complex is binding rather than abandoning the asset class.
Ukrainian strikes reaching 1,300 kilometers into Russia have taken out nearly half of Russian refining capacity, forcing the world's supposedly largest product and crude exporter to import jet fuel from Japan and diesel from India.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highRussiaUkrainejet fueldieselJapanIndia
So what: Russia flipping from product exporter to importer removes a structural supply cushion that global product balances had assumed permanent.
Because Soviet-era Russian refineries have a single crude pipe feeding the CDU, knocking out the refinery also shuts in the upstream crude, with only about 35% of that crude redirectable and roughly eighteen months to rebuild the damaged units.
causal🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highRussiaCDUrefineriescrude oil
So what: Refinery damage in Russia is a crude supply loss too, so the bullish product story converts into a bullish crude story with a multi-quarter lag.
China's decision to cut refinery runs and refined product exports and draw down its own product stocks made global products tighter even though observers credited it with keeping prices down for Trump.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highChinarefined productsTrump
So what: The consensus read of Chinese behavior as price-suppressive is backwards for products; watch Chinese refinery run rates as a swing factor in diesel.
Product tankers could not be used to evacuate supply from the Arab Gulf because refined product ships effectively function as bombs when hit, unlike crude tankers which merely catch fire, so crude was shipped instead of the products the market needed.
causal🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highArab Gulfproduct tankersSaudi Arabiacrude oil
So what: Wartime shipping physics structurally biases the market toward crude surplus and product deficit - a persistent reason cracks stay elevated.
Hormuz Complacency and the Escalation Trap 6
Despite the Red Sea, Black Sea and Strait of Hormuz all being disrupted and roughly half of Russian refining destroyed, crude was still struggling to hold $100 a barrel, a combination Currie says would two years ago have implied global recession.
contrarian🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highStrait of HormuzRed SeaBlack SeaRussiacrude oil
So what: The gap between physical disruption and price is the trade - it implies asymmetric upside if the market is forced to reprice.
Currie argues the conditional probability of oil going to $150 once it clears $100 is now high because inventory buffers are depleted, unlike in prior cycles when the move was gradual.
prediction🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcrude oilBrent
So what: Call spreads or upside optionality above $100 are mispriced if the distribution is jump-like rather than continuous.
≈ “the conditional probability going to 150 once you get over to 100 is pretty high” paraphrase
The market's assumption that Trump will back down is flawed because he has no remaining concession to offer - the MOU was the rabbit he already pulled from the hat, and Iran will not supply crude on those terms again.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highTrumpIranMOU
So what: The de-escalation put that the macro community is implicitly short is much less valuable than priced.
Currie expects the current Middle East confrontation to be seen in retrospect as a Waterloo-style strategic defeat, with Iran gaining leverage every week that passes.
prediction🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highIranMiddle EastUnited States
So what: Positions should be sized for a prolonged, structurally worsening geopolitical premium rather than a resolution trade.
≈ “I think this is gonna be, uh, akin to Napoleon's Waterloo in retrospect” paraphrase
Even instant peace would not restore supply, because damaged Middle Eastern and Russian wells have water-cut and restart problems and negotiated supply contracts are being redirected from West to East.
causal🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highMiddle EastRussiaoil wells
So what: A ceasefire headline is a fade rather than a reason to exit long commodity exposure.
US and allied missile inventories are being drained fighting $40,000 drones with million-dollar Patriot interceptors, a trade Currie says should be replaced by F-15s and F-35s dropping gravity bombs and which he was told leaves the West in a dangerous position.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: mediumPatriot missilesdronesF-15F-35United States
So what: Munitions restocking is a multi-year defense-industrial demand stream and a further claim on metals and manufacturing capacity.
≈ “You cannot fight, um, $40,000 drones with million-dollar Patriot missiles.” paraphrase
The Physical Capital Paradox and State Capitalism 7
Commodities are the best performing asset class of the decade, up over 200% versus roughly 157% for crypto on average, dating from the bullish supercycle call Currie made in October 2020.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcommoditiescryptoGoldman Sachs
So what: Allocators who dismiss commodities on price-level optics have missed the highest-returning sleeve of the decade.
≈ “Commodities are the best performing asset class this decade. You know, you're up over 200%. Crypto on average is 157.” paraphrase
This cycle's commodity returns come from rolling a backwardated front end rather than price appreciation, so being long oil during the war generated about 40% returns even while oil sat near $60 and below its pre-war level.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcrude oilroll yieldbackwardationcontango
So what: Judge commodity exposure on total return including roll yield, not spot - a flat or falling price can still pay handsomely.
Energy is just under 4% of the S&P and energy plus basic materials is under 6%, roughly a third of the long-run average, so even a 100% move in energy adds only about 3% to a portfolio while tech at ~50% weight needs just 6% to match it.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highS&P 500energy sectorbasic materialstechnology sector
So what: Explains structural complacency: index-relative managers are rationally uninterested in commodities until an energy shock demolishes their tech-heavy returns as in 2022.
Dodd-Frank removed bank proprietary risk-taking from commodity markets, concentrating roughly 98% of liquidity in the first six months of the curve and leaving no counterparty to sell long-dated hedges, so the only practical way to buy oil 18 months out is to buy an oil company.
causal🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highDodd-Frankoil futuresbanksoil companies
So what: The absence of back-end liquidity means physical hedgers must use equities, and it mechanically converts commodity moves into spikes rather than trends.
Without back-end buying, commodity markets hit potholes and spike vertically instead of anticipating them, which Currie illustrates with Gerald Ford's ban on onion futures and with Newcastle coal doing nothing through the whole 2000s supercycle before going straight up in late 2008.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highonion futuresGerald FordNewcastle coalfutures markets
So what: Markets lacking deep forward curves should be traded for gap risk, not trend - a reason to own optionality rather than delta.
Energy companies are throwing off a 15% free cash flow yield, which Currie labels the 'munificent seven', yet capital still will not come until physical shortages and lines appear - as in the 1970s and the 2000s when BHP-type names rose 50x only after warehouse shelves emptied.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highenergy companiesBHPfree cash flow yield
So what: The catalyst for the commodity capital rotation is a visible physical shortage, not valuation - wait for empty shelves, then expect a violent flow.
≈ “People don't buy these stories until you run out, and then that capital flows.” paraphrase
Currie says state capitalism is back because the needed projects have roughly 27-year durations and billions in upfront cost that private investors will not tolerate, comparing it to the World War II conversion of auto plants into weapons plants.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highstate capitalismChinainfrastructurecopper mines
So what: Expect state-backed financing vehicles and offtake guarantees to become the dominant funding channel for energy and mining - a policy trade as much as a commodity one.
“I think state capitalism is back.” ▶ 1:16:18
The Abundance Illusion Playbook 4
Currie traces the Western policy of never admitting scarcity to Jimmy Carter's two 1977 speeches - the cardigan 'conserve' speech that triggered panic and higher prices, and the April speech coining 'energy transition' as the moral equivalent of war, mocked as MEOW.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highJimmy Carterenergy transitionMEOW
So what: Explains why policymakers systematically suppress rather than transmit scarcity signals, delaying the investment response and enlarging the eventual spike.
“Never admit the scarcity, 'cause he got killed.” ▶ 28:24
Every US president from George H.W. Bush through Trump has run the same abundance illusion - releasing the SPR and talking the market down to bridge to returning supply - and Trump needed it in his second term though not his first.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highSPRGeorge H.W. BushClintonObamaBidenTrump
So what: Reserve releases should be read as a bet on supply returning, not as a bearish fundamental - and the bet fails when the buffer runs out.
Drawing down strategic petroleum reserves liquidates a buffer built over decades in order to suppress the very price signal that would trigger the investment response the market needs.
causal🗣 Nate Hagensconviction: highSPRoil investment
So what: Policy-suppressed prices mean the supply response is delayed, making the eventual shortage deeper and the price move more violent.
China took Carter's energy transition logic seriously for security rather than environmental reasons and became the world leader in nuclear generation technology, solar, wind, batteries and lithium, which Currie says leaves it in a relatively good position now.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highChinanuclearsolarwindbatterieslithium
So what: The security framing, not the climate framing, is what mobilizes capital - watch for Western energy policy to be re-labeled as defense spending.
≈ “It heeded that advice and invested in it. It had nothing to do with the environment, but everything for energy security” paraphrase
Grand Bargain, Exorbitant Privilege and Bloc Fragmentation 6
The Bretton Woods grand bargain gave the US exorbitant privilege in exchange for using its navy to protect global sea lanes, and with the Red Sea, Strait of Hormuz and Black Sea all blocked simultaneously that bargain is visibly unfulfilled.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highBretton WoodsUS dollarWorld BankIMFStrait of HormuzRed SeaBlack Sea
So what: If the sea-lane guarantee is not restored, the credit flows that fund US consumption and asset prices are the thing at risk, not just oil.
Currie argues that if foreign capital stops trusting the US and pulls out because the grand bargain is broken, the adjustment falls on middle class American living standards.
prediction🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highUnited StatesUS dollarmiddle class
So what: The transmission channel from geopolitics to US households is capital flight and lost credit access, which argues for owning hard assets over US consumption exposure.
≈ “That's gonna hit middle class America and change their lifestyles like they've never seen before.” paraphrase
Trump's derided proposal for a 20% tax on oil transiting the Strait of Hormuz was an attempt to get paid for the expensive service of protecting sea lanes, and Currie's 'New Martiel Plan' argues that once interest rates rose the system became too expensive for any president to maintain.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: mediumTrumpStrait of Hormuzinterest rates
So what: The retreat from global policing is rate-driven and structural, so it survives changes of administration - price permanent risk premia into shipping and energy.
Currie frames the current conflict as BRICS versus G7 with Europeans not realizing they are participants, bookending the 1991 Gulf War moment when the Soviet Union fell and the US won overwhelmingly and became the globalization hegemon.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highBRICSG7Soviet UnionGulf WarChina
So what: Treat 1991 and 2026 as the open and close of the globalization trade, with the reverse of every asset that benefited from it.
≈ “this is BRICS versus G7, even though the Europeans don't know” paraphrase
China controls the world's atoms through critical minerals processing that the West and even the Soviets refused to do domestically, controls the world's molecules through its backing of Iran, and would control the world's chips if it takes Taiwan.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highChinacritical mineralsIranTaiwansemiconductorsRussia
So what: A single actor holding all three chokepoints makes supply-chain redundancy a national-security capex cycle rather than a corporate choice.
≈ “So they control the world's atoms, they control the world's molecules, and also the strait, um, Taiwan to get them into Taiwan, um, then they control the world's chips” paraphrase
Currie's worst realistic case short of nuclear use is Putin choosing to cut the West off from Russian commodities entirely, which he calls a non-trivial probability given Russia is the only one of the three largest producers with both metals and energy while the US has energy without metals and China metals without energy.
prediction🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: mediumRussiaPutinChinaUnited Statescritical minerals
So what: A Russian export embargo would shut down Western industrial processes - a tail risk worth hedging in metals rather than only in oil.
≈ “Russia has both metals and energy. China has metals, but no energy.” paraphrase
Scarcity Versus Debasement: The Second Supercycle 7
Currie's framework for the next decade is a rotation between two themes - scarcity, headlined by oil, copper and an agricultural proxy like corn or soybeans, and debasement, headlined by gold.
framework🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highgoldoilcoppercornsoybeans
So what: A simple two-factor allocation map for the decade: rotate between the scarcity basket and the debasement basket rather than picking one.
≈ “You're gonna rotate between the thematics of scarcity and debasement.” paraphrase
Currie cites the levels already reached - gold having hit $5,500 and trading around $4,200, diesel at $160, Brent around $101 and copper at $14,000 a ton - and says these are only the foothills of the Himalayas relative to what is coming.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highgolddieselBrentcopper
So what: Anchors for how far the complex has already moved and a claim that the distribution of further upside is still fat.
≈ “we're only in the foothills of the Himalayas at this point” paraphrase
Supply response is structurally impossible on the relevant timeframe because a copper mine takes roughly twenty years to bring on, no new refineries have been built, and current Brazilian and Guyanese oil growth is the output of the previous investment cycle.
causal🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highcopperBrazilGuyanarefineriesoil
So what: Bearish supply arguments based on current production growth are backward-looking; the forward pipeline is empty.
Goldman's regularly published list of top oil projects had, in its most recent version, no major new projects for the first time ever, a direct legacy of the sustainability-driven halt to oil investment earlier in the decade.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highGoldman Sachsoil projectssustainability
So what: A concrete, checkable indicator that the supply cliff is already locked in for the back half of the decade.
The AI build-out represents roughly $800 billion of spending this year with more than 50% flowing directly into commodities - metals, turbines, transformers - against zero incremental investment in the raw materials required.
data_point🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highAIcopperturbinestransformersdata centers
So what: Currie calls it the biggest demand shock commodities have ever seen and says exaggeratedly that all of it ends up in the price of copper.
Currie sees significant upside in agriculture from the combination of El Nino heat, Black Sea and Odessa port disruption to Russian and Ukrainian grain, a fertilizer shock arriving in 2027, and the exhaustion of the strong-dollar incentive that drove Latin American acreage expansion.
thesis🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: highEl NinograinOdessaBlack SeafertilizerLatin AmericaUS dollar
So what: He owns the whole commodity index rather than only oil precisely because ags and metals carry independent shocks.
Currie concedes peak oil supply may already be upon us because the extent of damage to Russian and Middle Eastern productive capacity is unknown, which would force the electrification transition far faster.
prediction🗣 Jeff Currieconviction: mediumpeak oilRussiaMiddle Eastbatteries
So what: Supply-side peak oil is a live scenario that would compress the energy transition timeline and reprice both oil and battery supply chains.
≈ “If you took out Russia and the Middle East, well, folks, you, you better learn how to make those batteries really quick” paraphrase
Fiscal Transfers, Credit and Sovereign Fragility 6
Currie argues inflation is caused by fiscal transfers to low-income households rather than by monetary policy, noting twenty years of interest rate cuts produced no inflation while Biden-era $2,000 checks made commodity prices explode.
