“So Google, Meta, Microsoft, and Amazon since they all reported curiously on the same day back April 29th and their stock prices just fell throughout the course of the quarter after raising capital spending.” ▶ 29:14
Knapp benchmarks capex-to-EBITDA against prior peaks - 80% in telecommunications in 1999-2000 and a similar peak in energy during the 2014-15 shale boom - and says the big AI spenders are already at 90%.
framework🗣 Barry Knappconviction: hightelecommunications sectorenergy sectorshalehyperscalerscapex-to-EBITDA
So what: Gives a concrete, historically calibrated metric showing AI capex intensity has already exceeded two prior bubble peaks.
“we're actually at if you look at the big spenders we're at 90% right now capex to cash flow” ▶ 30:03
Even Google, which Knapp says has made the most progress monetizing its AI investment, fell 7% the day after reporting negative free cash flow for the quarter.
data_point🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highGooglefree cash flow
So what: If the best monetizer gets punished for negative FCF, weaker hyperscalers face worse reactions - watch FCF inflection dates as event risk.
“the reaction to them having negative cash flow for the quarter was pretty pretty profound, right? A 7% decline the next day”
Knapp expects capex to keep growing but not accelerate, and expects the slowing rate of change to permeate outward from the hyperscalers through the rest of the technology sector.
prediction🗣 Barry Knappconviction: hightechnology sectorAI capexsemiconductors
So what: Second-derivative-sensitive names - semis, networking, power suppliers - de-rate before revenue actually declines.
“I don't think capex is going to get cut. I just don't think it's going to rise as rapidly.” ▶ 30:31
Positioning: Underweight Tech and Consumer, Overweight Non-AI Capex 6
Knapp has cut his technology sector weight to 25% against a 37% index weight, describing it as a very large active underweight for a book followed by both institutional and individual investors.
thesis🗣 Barry Knappconviction: hightechnology sectorS&P 500Ironsides Macroeconomics
So what: A 12-point underweight from a macro strategist is a concrete, sizable expression of the capex-inflection view that others can benchmark against.
“I've reduced my exposure to the technology sector. The index weight is 37%. I'm at 25.” ▶ 0:17
Knapp is also half weight communication services - 5% versus a 10% index weight - noting the sector is effectively just two companies.
thesis🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highcommunication services sectorGoogleMeta
So what: Combined with the tech underweight, this is a broad de-risking of the mega-cap AI complex rather than a single-name call.
“for the communication services sector, which is really just two companies, the market weight's 10, I'm at five” ▶ 30:57
Knapp argues that holding 50% of a portfolio in one theme borders on recklessness, an implicit critique of benchmark-hugging investors forced into concentrated AI exposure by index construction.
framework🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highS&P 500AI themeindex concentration
So what: Frames index-tracking itself as an active concentration bet, an argument for equal-weight or ex-mega-cap exposure.
“In my view, if you have 50% of your portfolio in one theme, that borders on recklessness” ▶ 31:13
Knapp is overweight industrials, energy, materials and financials, betting on a manufacturing renaissance and non-AI capital investment plus the bank-friendly effects of Warsh's deregulation and yield-curve steepening.
thesis🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highindustrialsenergymaterialsfinancialsmanufacturing renaissanceKevin Warsh
So what: A direct pair trade against the AI complex: long the broadening capex cycle, short the concentrated one.
“underweight the consumer, overweight capital spending, but not AI capital spending, everything else.” ▶ 32:53
Knapp has held very low weights in consumer staples and consumer discretionary since before Liberation Day and leaned into that underweight afterward because of tariff and energy-driven margin pressure plus the K-shaped consumer.
thesis🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highconsumer staplesconsumer discretionarytariffsLiberation Day
So what: The stated trigger to reverse is the Fed definitively unwinding its imbalanced policy - a specific, observable entry signal for consumer names.
“When we get to the point where the Fed is definitively unwinding this imbalanced policy they have then I would look to get back more deeply exposed to the consumer companies.” ▶ 32:11
Knapp holds a decent cash position because he believes rising real rates could trigger a 10% drawdown at any point, noting such drawdowns typically happen in midterm years and specifically flagging August as the likely window.
prediction🗣 Barry Knappconviction: mediumreal interest ratesUS equitiesmidterm elections
So what: A dated, sized risk call: hold dry powder into an August real-rate-driven equity correction in a midterm year.
“Got a decent cash position because I think that rising real rates could cause, you know, a 10% draw down at any point.” ▶ 0:23
Where Investors Are Offsides 5
Knapp says the AI capex slowdown is no longer a blind spot because investors have already woken up to it, and that high-multiple semiconductor names like Micron already reflect worry that current earnings will not persist beyond a year or two.
contrarian🗣 Barry Knappconviction: mediumMicronsemiconductorsAI capex
So what: Warns against treating the AI-capex-slowdown trade as fresh alpha - the crowding has already happened in semis.
Knapp identifies consumer staples as the biggest genuine mispricing: valuations remain very high - he cites Walmart's P/E and roughly 30 times earnings - while sector earnings growth is only about 5% and margins are under pressure.
contrarian🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highWalmartconsumer staplesP/E ratios
So what: Names a specific expensive defensive trade to avoid or fade even after underperformance.
“why are you paying 30 times earnings for something for earnings are going up 5%” ▶ 34:44
Knapp argues buying a slow-growing company at 25 times earnings is not a defensive trade but a silly one, and attributes staples' persistent premium to investors who refuse to leave the market and default to 'getting defensive.'
framework🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highconsumer staplesdefensive equities
So what: Reframes valuation, not sector label, as the source of downside protection - relevant when positioning for his forecast 10% drawdown.
“buying a slow growing company at 25 times earnings is not really a defensive trade. It's kind of a silly trade.” ▶ 34:57
Knapp concedes industrials are expensive because the crowd already agrees with his long capex cycle and manufacturing renaissance thesis, but says the crowd is probably right and investors will earn it out over time.
thesis🗣 Barry Knappconviction: mediumindustrialsmanufacturing renaissance
So what: A rare case where a strategist stays long a consensus, richly valued trade - expect time-based rather than re-rating-based returns.
Knapp believes regional banks remain cheap despite strong performance this year because investors do not fully appreciate how much the coming regulatory regime and yield-curve steepening will boost their profitability.
contrarian🗣 Barry Knappconviction: highregional banksfinancialsyield curvebank deregulation
So what: This is his clearest stated edge - a still-underpriced link between the Warsh policy plan and regional bank earnings.
“I don't think people fully appreciate how much this regulatory regime and steeping of the steepening of the yield curve will mean for their profitability.” ▶ 35:44

The Lawfare Podcast 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

Lawfare Daily: Dispatches from the 2026 Aspen Security Forum39 min32 insights2026-07-29
▶ Watch on YouTube
Four attendees of the July 14-17, 2026 Aspen Security Forum (Mark Leon Goldberg, Cory Shaki, Alex Zerden, Alexandra Bell) report that the dominant storyline was a closed Strait of Hormuz and an Iran war widely judged a disaster, with the on-stage establishment consensus still prescribing more military pressure. The most investable threads are Zerden's warning that the buffers holding oil prices down (Chinese demand destruction and reserve draws) are nearing exhaustion, his skepticism that USTR Greer's assumption of an indestructible US consumer holds, the post-Supreme Court pivot from IEEPA tariffs to Sections 301/232, and a stranded-fertilizer food-price shock rippling through the Horn of Africa. The Trump administration sent almost no security officials, leaving the forum dominated by private sector, civil society, foreign governments, and retired officials.
Hormuz Closure and the Iran Quagmire · 7Oil, Food and the Exhausting Buffers · 4Tariff Authorities After the IEEPA Ruling · 3Nuclear Risk and Hollowed-Out Arms Control · 4The Administration's No-Show at Aspen · 4Bipartisanship, Norms and US Legal Legitimacy · 3Off-Agenda Conflicts and Illicit Finance · 5Pakistan and Turkey as Swing Middle Powers · 2
Hormuz Closure and the Iran Quagmire 7
At the 2026 Aspen Security Forum's opening panel, Conda Rice, Ambassador Nick Burns, Penny Pritsker and former Defense Secretary Mark Esper converged on the view that the US cannot let Iran control the Strait of Hormuz, and the only prescription offered was more military pressure, with no pushback until Senator Chris Murphy took the stage later.
data_point🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highIranHormuzConda RiceNick BurnsPenny PritskerMark EsperChris MurphyAspen Security Forum
So what: The US foreign policy establishment's revealed preference is escalation rather than a negotiated reopening, so investors should not price in a near-term diplomatic de-escalation of the Hormuz shipping disruption.
“is it really like the state of quote you know establishment thought right now that the straight of Hormuz can be opened through the application of military force” ▶ 3:28
Mark Leon Goldberg's takeaway from Aspen is that there is no solution to the Hormuz crisis and the strait will remain closed for the foreseeable future, because the military pressure now being advertised as the fix is what closed it in the first place.
prediction🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highHormuzIranoil
So what: A prolonged Hormuz closure is a persistent tail risk for energy, tanker rates, marine insurance and fertilizer/food supply chains rather than a transitory headline event.
“there is no solution to the hormuz crisis and you know uh that you know it will remain closed for the foreseeable future” ▶ 31:32
Goldberg offers a recurring quagmire framework from 20 years covering Iraq and Afghanistan: when a policy is failing, policymakers' knee-jerk impulse is to escalate rather than exit, drawing the country deeper into uncertain outcomes, and Iran is the latest instance.
framework🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highIraqAfghanistanIranforeign policy
So what: A reusable base rate for handicapping US conflict paths: default expectation should be gradual escalation and duration extension, not resolution, which favors longer-dated risk premia in energy and defense.
“there is always a consistent line of thought that compels policymakers to want to throw more resources at a failing problem” ▶ 4:08
Cory Shaki argues the 2025 Iran war had a clear objective largely fulfilled by the 12-day war, while the 2026 choices made by Israel and the United States were much sloppier, under-resourced and not thought through, a distinction she says was missing from Aspen's blanket consensus that the whole undertaking was a disaster.
contrarian🗣 Cory Shakiconviction: highIranIsraelUnited StatesAspen Security Forum
So what: If the failure is specific to 2026 execution rather than the entire campaign, the policy correction (and any de-escalation trade) hinges on 2026 decision-makers, not a wholesale reversal of the Iran strategy.
“the choices that both Israel and the United States made in 2026, which I think were much sloppier, much less well resourced, and very clearly not thought through” ▶ 5:30
Alex Zerden reports that former Defense Secretary Mark Esper told the Aspen audience, in Zerden's paraphrase, that economic tools are effectively the only options left against Iran because military options have not succeeded to date, and that sanctions are being reinjected into both the Iran and Russia conversations.
thesis🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumMark EsperIranRussiaUS Treasury Departmenteconomic sanctions
So what: Expect the next phase of Iran and Russia policy to be sanctions-led, raising compliance, secondary-sanctions and payment-rail risk for firms with Gulf, Russian or crypto exposure.
“the economic options are really the only options left to challenge and contest Iran, that the military options have not succeeded to date” ▶ 6:03
Alexandra Bell warns that Iran hovered at breakout capability for years without deciding to build a weapon, that the US does not know where all the fissile material is (something the Trump administration's own intelligence community has alluded to), and that the strikes may have pushed Tehran into finally deciding it needs a nuclear capacity, which short of a ground invasion and occupation could not be stopped.
thesis🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highIranNorth KoreaTrump administrationintel communitynuclear capacity
So what: A weaponization decision by Iran would reprice regional escalation risk, Gulf security guarantees and proliferation cascades far beyond the current Hormuz disruption.
“did we push the Iranian regime into into finally deciding that yes, they do need a nuclear capacity” ▶ 7:34
Cory Shaki, who holds the Kissinger chair at the Library of Congress and is writing a book on why the United States cannot win wars, concluded from the Aspen panels that the Trump administration has not found a new American way of war but only new ways to lose American wars.
contrarian🗣 Cory Shakiconviction: highTrump administrationmilitaryLibrary of CongressIran
So what: A credibility-erosion thesis from an AEI defense-policy lead signals that allied hedging and adversary risk-taking are likely to increase, a slow-burn negative for US-anchored security assumptions.
“the Trump administration hasn't found a new American way of war. They've just found new ways to lose American wars.” ▶ 33:44
Oil, Food and the Exhausting Buffers 4
Oil has stayed within a relatively stable price band since the Iran war began, with no $200 barrels, because Chinese demand destruction and reserve draws absorbed the shock, but Alex Zerden says the sidebar consensus at Aspen is that this resiliency is nearing its end and major price spikes in oil and other products transiting Hormuz are a real risk factor.
prediction🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumoilChinaHormuzIranreserves
So what: The single most tradeable claim in the episode: the buffer that has kept crude range-bound is depleting, arguing for upside convexity in energy and for hedging refined-product and freight exposure.
“We haven't gotten $200 barrels of oil as a result of the war” ▶ 16:52
Zerden says China presents an opaque or incomplete picture of its oil reserves and reserve capabilities, that there is consensus the reserves have decreased, and that despite years of nuclear reactor buildout and its position as a huge producer and consumer of solar and other renewables, China remains heavily reliant on imported oil.
data_point🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumChinaoilnuclear powersolarrenewablesreserves
So what: Chinese reserve depth is the key unknown determining how long global crude stays capped; watch Chinese import and stockpile data as the leading indicator for the next leg in oil.
“How how deep are their reserves? That's the live question.” ▶ 17:55
Goldberg identifies a second-order food shock from the Hormuz closure: fertilizer that the Horn of Africa depends on has been stranded on the wrong side of the strait through an entire planting season, so global food prices that are already high are poised to go much higher with destabilizing knock-on effects.
causal🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highHormuzfertilizerHorn of Africafood pricesgas prices
So what: A missed planting season creates a lagged, hard-to-reverse agricultural supply shortfall, arguing for exposure to fertilizer and soft commodities and for political-instability risk in import-dependent frontier markets.
“fertilizer that they normally depend on that transits the straight of horm has been stuck on the wrong side through the course of an entire planting season” ▶ 32:05
Zerden frames the current environment as 'permacrisis' - a sequence of COVID supply-chain and public-health shocks, the Russia-Ukraine disruption to oil, gas and fertilizer molecules with downstream food-production effects on poor countries, and now the Iran war - and argues it is a management problem that will never be solved rather than a crisis to be resolved.
framework🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highCOVIDRussiaUkraineIransupply chainsfertilizerfood prices
So what: Treats disruption as a permanent operating regime, favoring companies and portfolios built for resilience and optionality over those priced on mean-reverting stability.
“It's it's a management problem. It's not going to be solved ever.” ▶ 31:05
Tariff Authorities After the IEEPA Ruling 3
Following a Supreme Court decision nullifying the use of the International Emergency Economic Powers Act for certain tariff measures, US Trade Representative Jameson Greer told Aspen he is optimistic about the administration's remaining authorities under Section 301 and Section 232 and other ways to deploy tariffs.
data_point🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highJameson GreerUS Trade RepresentativeSupreme CourtInternational Emergency Economic Powers ActSection 301Section 232tariffs
So what: The tariff regime is being re-plumbed rather than dismantled, so importers should model Section 301/232 sector- and country-specific actions rather than assume broad tariff relief from the court ruling.
“ambassador Greer seems optimistic about the their authorities under section 301, section 232” ▶ 14:58
Alex Zerden flags that Jameson Greer's trade framework treats the resilience of the US consumer as a constant, and argues it is better understood as a variable given recent inflation numbers, supply-chain shocks and potential AI displacement of the labor force.
contrarian🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumJameson GreerUS consumerinflationAIlabor forcetariffs
So what: The core assumption underpinning aggressive tariff policy is consumer absorption capacity; if consumption cracks under inflation or AI-driven job displacement, tariff policy and consumer-exposed equities both reprice.
“he has a lot of confidence in the certainty of the resilience of the US consumer” ▶ 18:40
Greer used his Aspen remarks to plant a flag about Chinese supply chain vulnerabilities without elaborating, which Zerden called an interesting nugget and speculated may be intimating China's own oil dependence.
thesis🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: exploratoryJameson GreerChinasupply chainscritical mineraloil
So what: A hint that Washington is preparing to press Chinese supply-chain and energy-import vulnerabilities rather than only defend US ones, which would open a new front in the trade conflict.
“there are Chinese supply chain concerns that exist and he didn't go into too much detail about it” ▶ 16:29
Nuclear Risk and Hollowed-Out Arms Control 4
Alexandra Bell, formerly at the State Department, says the president fired all of the State Department staff working on multilateral nuclear arms control despite repeatedly saying he wants to reduce nuclear threats, and that she is not even sure trained nuclear experts are in the ongoing Iran dialogues because the administration is not transparent about who attends.
causal🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highState DepartmentTrump administrationnuclear arms controlIran
So what: Loss of technical negotiating capacity lowers the probability of any verifiable Iran or Russia nuclear deal, extending the geopolitical risk premium rather than resolving it.
“he fired all of the people at the State Department working on multilateral nuclear arms control” ▶ 13:29
Bell characterizes the present as the most dangerous nuclear situation since the end of the Cold War with everything in the nuclear space trending in the wrong direction, yet says nuclear issues received minimal treatment at the Aspen Security Forum and were touched only briefly when Iran or Russia came up.
thesis🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highnuclearRussiaIranAspen Security ForumBulletin of the Atomic Scientists
So what: A risk the policy community itself is under-discussing is by definition under-priced by markets, making nuclear escalation an unhedged tail.
“Everything in the nuclear space is trending in the wrong direction.” ▶ 23:55
Bell notes that China is rapidly expanding its nuclear arsenal in an opaque and dangerous fashion while refusing strategic stability dialogues and intimidating regional neighbors, and yet Europeans now view China more favorably than before, which she calls a reality check on how much trust the United States has lost.
contrarian🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highChinaEuropeUnited Statesnuclear arsenalstrategic stability
So what: European sentiment drifting toward China despite Chinese nuclear expansion signals durable transatlantic realignment risk for supply chains, export controls and alliance-dependent trades.
“it's also surprising to me that that people would give trust to a country that's given them no reason to trust them” ▶ 33:17
Bell reports that climate change never came up at the 2026 Aspen Security Forum and biological risks did not come up once despite the world being less than five years past the most disruptive biological event in a hundred years, though one panel did cover the security implications of AI.
data_point🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highclimate changebiological risksCOVIDAIAspen Security ForumBulletin of the Atomic Scientists
So what: Attention has rotated wholesale from climate and pandemic risk to AI and kinetic conflict, a sentiment shift that affects where security-adjacent policy money and mandates flow next.
“I haven't heard biological risks come up at all.” ▶ 23:12
The Administration's No-Show at Aspen 4
The Trump administration sent no Department of Defense representatives and only limited participation overall to the 2026 Aspen Security Forum, with Ambassador Greer among the few attendees, in contrast to Biden-era forums that drew the CIA director, National Security Adviser Sullivan and the secretary of state.
data_point🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highDepartment of DefenseTrump administrationBiden administrationJameson GreerCIASullivanAspen Security Forum
So what: Reduced official participation lowers the informational value of these forums as a read on actual US policy, forcing analysts to rely more on formal announcements and less on off-record signaling.
“no representatives uh from the department of defense” ▶ 9:22
Goldberg traces the drop-off to last year, when the defense secretary barred military officials from attending at the last minute with a knock-on chilling effect on other administration officials, and notes the administration did the same to the Halifax International Security Conference held in Canada each November.
causal🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: mediumDepartment of DefenseHalifax International Security ConferenceAspen Security ForumCanada
So what: A systematic policy of disengagement from Western security conferences, not a one-off, so expect thinner official signal from Halifax and similar venues going forward.
Cory Shaki attributes the absence of US security officials to unwillingness to hear about the disappointment their policies are fostering and to the MAGA wing's resentment of elite consensus-building forums, while noting that a notable number of retired military officers and retired national security career officials showed up to sustain the policy dialogue.
causal🗣 Cory Shakiconviction: highTrump administrationRepublican PartyDepartment of DefenseDepartment of StateAspen Security Forum
So what: The formal policy channel and the expert channel are decoupling, meaning expert consensus is a weaker predictor of actual US action than in prior administrations.
“this is a security forum and there was nobody from the security departments of the United States government. And I think it's because they don't want to hear the disappointment and the challenges that their policies are fostering.” ▶ 9:42
Zerden argues from experience inside the administration that governing burns down intellectual capital every day through the tyranny of the inbox, so outside interaction with civil society and academia - even bad ideas - improves policy, and that low administration turnout shifted this year's Aspen composition toward the private sector, civil society, foreign governments and journalists as speakers rather than moderators.
framework🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highUS governmentcivil societyacademiaprivate sectorAspen Security Forum
So what: Policy quality degrades when the outside-expertise loop is cut, raising the odds of unforced errors and abrupt policy reversals that markets have to absorb without warning.
“you're burning down your intellectual capital every day. Uh just the process of governing, the tyranny of the inbox” ▶ 11:17
Bipartisanship, Norms and US Legal Legitimacy 3
A forum attendee highlighted Carl Rove's on-stage rejection of Vice President Vance's blood-and-soil approach to American citizenship and his argument that this is not the most dangerous time in American politics, citing earlier eras that were more violent and contested, calling the message weirdly comforting and hopeful about the political system's tools to handle contestation.
anecdote🗣 forum attendeeconviction: mediumCarl RoveVanceRepublican PartyAmerican politics
So what: Evidence of an intra-Republican split on nationalism and a case that US institutional resilience is being underrated, relevant to how much political-risk discount to apply to US assets.
“Carl Rove was guns blazing with his rejection of Vice President Vance's blood and soil approach to American citizenship” ▶ 0:05
Alexandra Bell criticizes a reticence at Aspen to name the real problems - lip service to bipartisan solutions without discussing actual solutions, and tiptoeing around the administration's undermining of democratic principles and of international structures the US spent decades building in its own national security and economic interest.
thesis🗣 Alexandra Bellconviction: highTrump administrationinternational structuresUS national securityAspen Security Forum
So what: If the expert community self-censors on institutional erosion, public discourse will lag the actual deterioration, meaning institutional risk gets recognized late and repriced abruptly.
“We can pay lip service to, you know, bipartisan solutions, but we actually have to talk about the solutions” ▶ 0:33
A Lawfare-affiliated participant argues the missing layer at Aspen was legal: US moves to undermine bodies like the International Criminal Court and to cast aside international legal norms when they are inconvenient, combined with domestic legal turmoil, erode the image of a fair and just society and hand Russia and China the argument that the US is just like them.
causal🗣 Lawfare-affiliated participantconviction: highInternational Criminal Courtinternational lawRussiaChinaUnited States
So what: Legitimacy erosion is a compounding cost to US soft power and coalition-building capacity, weakening the enforceability of sanctions and export-control regimes that depend on allied cooperation.
“the US isn't actually what it says it is. They're just like us.” ▶ 29:04
Off-Agenda Conflicts and Illicit Finance 5
Goldberg identifies Sudan as the largest humanitarian crisis in the world today, with the Rapid Support Forces militia massing outside the city of El Oed and poised to sack it, and notes that in every city the militia has taken over three years of conflict it has committed mass atrocities, making this a looming mass atrocity event.
prediction🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highSudanRapid Support ForcesEl Oedhumanitarian crisis
So what: A near-term, forecastable atrocity event with sanctions and diplomatic-crisis potential that is currently absent from the security-forum agenda and therefore from market attention.
“there is a genocidal militia called the rapid support forces which is massing outside of city a city called El Oed” ▶ 19:56
Goldberg says Sudan's civil war is to a large degree fueled and funded by outside powers - the United Arab Emirates, Egypt and Saudi Arabia - giving it an international security dimension that connects it directly to the Gulf conversations happening at Aspen.
causal🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highSudanUnited Arab EmiratesEgyptSaudi ArabiaRapid Support Forces
So what: Gulf state sponsorship makes Sudan a potential sanctions and reputational flashpoint for UAE, Egyptian and Saudi counterparties, not merely a humanitarian story.
Goldberg reports Myanmar's civil war passed a grim milestone of 100,000 conflict-related deaths earlier this month, making it by far the deadliest conflict in Asia, and that China recently entered the conflict in a more decisive way with unclear objectives and unclear consequences for its relations with India.
data_point🗣 Mark Leon Goldbergconviction: highMyanmarChinaIndiaAsia
So what: Direct Chinese intervention on the Myanmar border is a geopolitical development with China-India implications that is being ignored by the foreign policy establishment and therefore not priced.
“passed a grim milestone of 100,000 conflict related deaths earlier this month. By far the deadliest conflict in Asia.” ▶ 20:51
Zerden says the largest bank robbery in the world occurred last year without breaking into popular consciousness: North Korean actors hacked a cryptocurrency exchange in the UAE, and the proceeds are funding both North Korea's and Russia's war machines, while North Korea itself remains an evergreen existential threat to South Korea and Japan that nobody can build a conference panel around.
data_point🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highNorth KoreaUnited Arab Emiratescryptocurrency exchangeRussiaSouth KoreaJapanChina
So what: State-scale crypto theft is now a sanctions-evasion and war-financing channel, raising custody, exchange-security and AML risk for digital-asset venues, especially in the Gulf.
“they hacked into a cryptocurrency exchange in the UAE and it's funding their war machine, it's funding Russia's war machine” ▶ 26:20
Zerden argues overseas scam centers running romance and confidence schemes at hypercharged scale using digital assets are stealing Americans' entire life savings, driving bankruptcies, mortgages taken against savings and 401(k) draws, that the administration has taken some reasonable steps against it, and that this cross-cutting multi-jurisdictional issue deserves far more attention at forums like Aspen.
thesis🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: highscam centerscryptocurrencydigital assetsfinancial fraud401ksTrump administration
So what: Household-level crypto fraud losses are a rising regulatory catalyst; expect more enforcement and compliance obligations on payment rails and digital-asset intermediaries.
“stealing people's entire life savings making people go bankrupt having people take out mortgages” ▶ 27:08
Pakistan and Turkey as Swing Middle Powers 2
Zerden says Pakistan surfaced at Aspen as a key node and interlocutor in the US-Iran negotiations, with some of the talks having occurred in Pakistan, while simultaneously carrying unresolved issues from its most recent war with India, exposure through Pakistani migrant workers in the Gulf hit by the Iran war, a strategic-depth security umbrella it provides to certain Gulf states, and its own status as a nuclear power.
thesis🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumPakistanIranIndiaGulfnuclearmigrant workers
So what: Pakistan is an under-watched single point of failure connecting the Iran negotiation track, Gulf security guarantees and South Asian nuclear escalation risk.
Zerden flags Turkey as the other middle power that surfaced on the Aspen sidelines: active in the US-Iran war dynamics, in tension with Israel including a contest between the two in Syria, expanding its role in Africa, and, though a NATO ally, taking steps after a recent summit that may concern US policymakers and complicate implementation of existing sanctions regimes.
thesis🗣 Alex Zerdenconviction: mediumTurkeyIsraelSyriaNATOIranAfricasanctions
So what: Turkey's divergence from US positions inside NATO creates sanctions-enforcement gaps and a live Israel-Turkey contest in Syria that could become the next regional escalation vector.
“they're NATO NATO ally but coming even coming out of the Ankor summit you know doing some things that may be concerning to US policy makers” ▶ 35:49
